## Chapter 1 Introduction

## 1.1 Overview

Many human-centric environments—such as education, healthcare, and personalized support systems—can be formulated as sequential decision-making problems, where an intelligent agent must determine what action to take and when to take it over time. These settings are naturally modeled using Reinforcement Learning (RL), which optimizes long-term outcomes through trial-and-error interaction with an environment (Sutton and Barto 2018). In an Intelligent Tutoring System (ITS), for example, a pedagogical agent must decide whether to present a student with a worked example, prompt independent problem solving, or engage in collaborative construction—adapting to each student’s evolving knowledge state across dozens of steps. In clinical settings, a treatment agent must select interventions for a patient whose condition changes over time. Despite the considerable success of RL and Deep RL (DRL) in simulated domains, their adoption in real-world, human-facing applications remains limited by two longstanding challenges.

The first challenge is sample inefficiency. Classic DRL algorithms such as Deep Q-Networks (DQN) require millions of environment interactions to learn stable policies and generalize to unseen states. Yet interactions with humans—patients, students, users—are scarce, costly, and ethically constrained. Many prior studies on RL in educational settings attempt to derive pedagogical policies from fewer than 3,000 student–ITS interaction trajectories, and the resulting policies can prove ineffective (Shen and Chi 2016). The second challenge is reward function design. Designing an accurate reward function is a prerequisite for RL, yet it is notoriously difficult in human-centric domains where desired behaviors are complex, latent, and context-dependent (Kaelbling et al. 1996). Reward functions are typically hand-crafted, dependent on domain expertise, and prone to blind spots (Abbeel and Ng 2004; Goyal et al. 2019). Worse, misspecified rewards can produce policies that are inconsistent with intended goals (Amodei et al. 2016). Moreover, in sensitive human-centric domains—classrooms, clinics—live policy experimentation raises ethical concerns, making it infeasible to collect the exploratory data that RL methods require.

## 1.1.1 Apprenticeship Learning as an Alternative

Apprenticeship Learning (AL) offers a principled alternative by inferring the underlying reward function directly from expert demonstrations, enabling policy induction without explicit reward specification (Abbeel and Ng 2004). By assuming expert agents make optimal or near-optimal decisions, AL can efficiently approximate expert behavior from a limited number of demonstrated trajectories (Abbeel and Ng 2004; Ziebart et al. 2008). Classical AL approaches, however, are predominantly online: they require iterative interaction with the environment to collect new data and update the model (Ziebart et al. 2008; Ho and Ermon 2016b; Finn et al. 2016). This precludes their use in fully offline settings. Strictly offline AL methods—those that learn solely from pre-collected logs without any environment interaction—are therefore essential in human-centric domains. A recent advance, Energy-Based Distribution Matching (EDM) (Jarrett et al. 2020a), achieves strong performance in offline AL by matching state-action occupancy distributions between expert demonstrations and a learned policy using energy-based models, without requiring knowledge of model transitions or off-policy evaluations.

Despite EDM’s success, it—like most offline AL methods—rests on a sequence of idealized assumptions that are routinely violated in practice. This dissertation progressively relaxes these assumptions, developing a series of frameworks that address the realistic complexities of human demonstration data across educational and clinical settings.

## 1.1.2 Reward Modeling: Heterogeneous and Evolving Reward Functions

A foundational assumption of most offline AL methods is that all demonstrations share a single, homogeneous reward function that remains fixed throughout each trajectory. Both parts of this assumption are routinely violated in human-centric settings, and this dissertation addresses them in turn.

The first violation is cross-trajectory heterogeneity: different demonstrators pursue fundamentally different objectives. In an ITS, some students are learning-oriented while others prioritize efficiency; their pedagogical decisions reflect different underlying reward structures. Prior work addressing this, such as EM-IRL (Yang et al. 2020), is limited to small discrete state spaces (17 features). The first study of this dissertation adapts EM-EDM—an Expectation-Maximization framework built upon EDM—which simultaneously clusters student trajectories by their latent reward functions and induces cluster-specific policies, scaling to continuous state spaces with over 140 features. Even with as few as 24 expert demonstrations, EM-EDM outperforms four state-of-the-art AL baselines and two DRL baselines on predicting student pedagogical decisions.

The second violation is intra-trajectory evolution: even a single demonstrator’s goals shift across phases of a task. A student who begins a tutoring session cautiously requesting help may shift toward confident independent problem-solving as the session progresses; a clinician’s treatment priorities evolve as a patient’s condition changes. Such temporal dynamics cannot be captured by any static reward function. The second study leverages THEMES (Yang et al. 2025a)—a Time-aware Hierarchical EM Energy-based Subtrajectory AL framework—which automatically partitions expert trajectories into temporally coherent sub-trajectories, applies EM-EDM over each partition, and refines the segmentation through a hierarchically learned reward regulator. Applied across one educational and three clinical healthcare datasets, THEMES consistently outperforms all flat and hierarchical AL baselines, establishing that capturing temporally evolving reward functions substantially improves policy induction in human-centric domains.

## 1.1.3 Demonstration Quality: Imperfect and Negative Demonstrations

A second foundational assumption of AL is that all provided demonstrations are at minimum acceptable in quality—that imperfect trajectories should be discarded and failed ones carry no learnable signal. This dissertation challenges both parts of this assumption.

The first challenge concerns imperfect demonstrations: in practice, students explore, make errors, and revise strategies, producing trajectories of varying quality. Discarding all suboptimal data wastes the majority of available demonstrations; yet treating all as equally informative introduces noise that corrupts learned policies. The key insight is that imperfect demonstrations constitute structured signals—provided their relative quality is explicitly ranked. The third study introduces HALIDE (Hierarchical Apprenticeship Learning from Imperfect Demonstrations with Evolving Rewards), which integrates continuous quality signals—derived from Normalized Learning Gain—directly into the hierarchical policy induction process. HALIDE establishes a critical finding: incorporating suboptimal data without quality-aware ranking degrades performance, whereas ranked hierarchical learning enables principled reuse of mixed-quality trajectories and yields consistent gains in cross-semester generalization. Crucially, ranking in AL serves a fundamentally different role from reward functions in RL: rather than defining what is optimal, it modulates how much influence each demonstrated behavior exerts during learning.

The second challenge concerns decision-level importance: existing quality-weighting methods, including HALIDE, apply a single trajectory-level rank uniformly across every decision, even though most decisions occur at routine states while a minority occur at critical junctures where the choice made is pivotal to the outcome—and failed trajectories are typically discarded entirely, even though they encode informative contrast about which decisions distinguish success from failure. The fourth study introduces CCHAL (Critical Contrast Hierarchical Apprenticeship Learning), which jointly attributes trajectory-level demonstration quality to individual decisions via Bayesian credit assignment in feature-action space, separately scores each decision’s criticality by contrasting expert against non-expert behavior within the same initial risk stratum, and combines both signals to sharpen a Bradley-Terry pairwise contrastive IRL objective over observed failure trajectories. Evaluated across three clinical EHR datasets and a student ITS dataset, CCHAL outperforms flat and hierarchical AL baselines, including its direct predecessor HALIDE, establishing that decision-level outcome attribution and behavioral criticality are both necessary to fully exploit demonstration quality signals.

## 1.1.4 Real-World Classroom Validation

The progression from EM-EDM through THEMES, HALIDE, and CCHAL addresses increasingly realistic data conditions in retrospective evaluation. The fifth study closes the loop with a real-world classroom deployment. StuEDM is a pedagogical policy in which students make their own decisions about how to engage with each training problem—choosing to solve it independently, study a worked example, or collaborate with the tutor—and an EDM-derived policy, trained on only a limited set of expert demonstrations, intervenes only when it disagrees with the student’s decision. In a controlled classroom study with 104 undergraduate students, StuEDM—trained on 244 students’ own pedagogical decisions across five semesters—is compared against StuCQL, an analogous policy whose override logic is provided by Conservative Q-Learning trained on a much larger, longer-span historical interaction corpus (2,716 students, 13 semesters), and against a pretest-equivalent Expert baseline that assigns among the same domain-expert-designed presentation modes with no adaptive guidance. StuEDM significantly improves posttest performance and normalized learning gain over the Expert baseline, and significantly improves the pretest-adjusted posttest score over StuCQL; a decision-level analysis further shows StuEDM overrides students’ own decisions less than half as often as StuCQL. StuEDM students also engaged with the tutor significantly longer than StuCQL students, a further engagement-related outcome favoring StuEDM, and the underlying training-corpus efficiency remains a genuine advantage, providing direct empirical evidence that offline AL from a substantially smaller, agency-driven demonstration corpus can rival reward-optimizing DRL trained on far larger datasets in real educational deployments.

## 1.2 Research Questions

RQ1: How can offline apprenticeship learning be used to model heterogeneous student pedagogical strategies?
Students exhibit diverse learning approaches and latent objectives, meaning a single monolithic pedagogical policy may not suit all learners. This question addresses the challenge of capturing such heterogeneity by adapting offline AL to simultaneously discover multiple latent reward functions and induce cluster-specific policies from continuous state spaces. By inducing policies directly from varied demonstration data, the aim is to uncover the distinct strategy types that different students follow without requiring reward labels or online experimentation.

RQ2: How can we capture the evolving nature of reward functions across different phases of sequential decision-making in education and healthcare?
Complex decision-making tasks unfold in distinct phases during which the decision-maker’s goals and trade-offs shift over time. Traditional RL and imitation learning assume a fixed reward function, which struggles to reflect these temporal shifts. RQ2 investigates how to automatically segment demonstration trajectories into sub-trajectories and infer phase-specific reward regimes from data, without requiring manually defined task boundaries—enabling policies that adapt to different decision-making stages in both educational and clinical domains.

RQ3: How can apprenticeship learning leverage imperfect human demonstrations while accounting for evolving reward structure and demonstration quality?
In real-world settings, purely expert demonstrations are rare. Available data consist largely of mixed-quality trajectories—partially successful behaviors, exploratory decisions, and non-optimal strategies. This question explores how to rank demonstrations by pedagogical quality and integrate that ranking into a hierarchical learning framework that preserves temporal structure and distinguishes transient errors from meaningful progress, enabling robust offline learning without reward labels or online interaction.

RQ4: Can decision-level outcome attribution and behavioral criticality, combined with contrastive learning from failed demonstrations, improve hierarchical apprenticeship learning beyond trajectory-level quality weighting?
Existing approaches to mixed-quality demonstration learning apply a single quality rank uniformly across all decisions within a trajectory, and typically discard failed demonstrations entirely. This question asks whether redistributing trajectory-level quality to individual decisions via Bayesian credit assignment, combined with a criticality score that contrasts expert against non-expert behavior at each decision, and a pairwise contrastive objective that treats observed failures as informative negatives, improves policy induction over trajectory-level quality weighting alone.

RQ5: Can offline apprenticeship learning from limited expert demonstrations outperform reward-optimizing deep RL trained on large data corpora in a real classroom deployment?
Advances in offline AL are typically evaluated retrospectively. This question asks whether the practical advantages of AL—no reward engineering, data efficiency, alignment with expert behavior—translate to superior student learning outcomes in a live classroom study, compared to a conservative deep RL policy trained on a large-scale interaction corpus.

## 1.3 Contributions

This dissertation contributes to the field of offline apprenticeship learning by advancing modeling capabilities across five interconnected dimensions:

1. 

Heterogeneous Reward Modeling (Chapter 3): We adapt the EM-EDM framework to educational data, discovering latent learner types and inducing cluster-specific pedagogical policies. This enables accurate modeling of diverse student strategies in large-scale tutoring logs with continuous state spaces, outperforming four AL baselines and two DRL baselines with as few as 24 expert demonstrations.

2. 

Evolving Reward Modeling (Chapter 4): We leverage THEMES, a time-aware hierarchical framework that automatically segments long-horizon trajectories into sub-trajectories governed by different latent objectives. Applied across one educational and three real-world clinical healthcare environments, THEMES captures temporal shifts in human strategies and substantially outperforms flat and hierarchical AL baselines.

3. 

Quality-Aware Hierarchical Learning from Imperfect Demonstrations (Chapter 5): We introduce HALIDE, a ranked hierarchical apprenticeship learning framework that integrates continuous demonstration quality signals into the policy induction process. HALIDE enables principled reuse of mixed-quality student data while preserving the temporal and strategic structure induced by hierarchical modeling, consistently improving over unweighted hierarchical baselines in cross-semester generalization.

4. 

Critical Contrast Hierarchical Learning from Imperfect Demonstrations (Chapter 6): We introduce CCHAL, which extends hierarchical AL by jointly attributing outcome quality to individual decisions via Bayesian credit assignment and scoring each decision’s behavioral criticality through a dual-arm contrastive comparison of expert against non-expert behavior, then sharpens reward learning with a Bradley-Terry pairwise contrastive IRL objective over observed failure trajectories. Evaluated on three clinical EHR datasets and one intelligent tutoring system dataset, CCHAL outperforms flat and hierarchical AL baselines, including its direct predecessor HALIDE, across educational and clinical domains.

5. 

Real-World Empirical Validation (Chapter 7): We deploy StuEDM—an EDM-induced policy that lets students make their own pedagogical decisions and intervenes only on disagreement, trained on an order-of-magnitude smaller demonstration corpus—in a controlled classroom study with 104 students. StuEDM significantly improves posttest performance and normalized learning gain over an Expert baseline that assigns among the same domain-expert-designed presentation modes with no adaptive guidance, and significantly improves the pretest-adjusted posttest score over CQL trained on a much larger historical corpus; StuEDM also overrides students’ own decisions less than half as often as CQL, providing direct empirical evidence that offline AL from a substantially smaller, agency-preserving demonstration corpus can rival reward-optimizing DRL in real educational settings.

Together, these contributions define a progression from modeling heterogeneous, to evolving, to imperfect, to contrastive reward structures, culminating in real-world empirical validation. They establish a unified framework for understanding and learning from complex human decision-making, with demonstrated applications in both education and healthcare.

The rest of the dissertation is organized as follows. Chapter 2 surveys relevant background and prior work. Chapter 3 addresses RQ1 through heterogeneous reward modeling via EM-EDM. Chapter 4 addresses RQ2 by leveraging THEMES for evolving reward functions across educational and clinical tasks. Chapter 5 addresses RQ3 through HALIDE, a quality-aware hierarchical framework for imperfect demonstrations. Chapter 6 addresses RQ4 through CCHAL, extending hierarchical AL with outcome-attributed decision weighting, dual-arm contrastive criticality scoring, and Bradley-Terry pairwise contrastive IRL. Chapter 7 addresses RQ5 through a live classroom deployment of StuEDM. Chapter 8 concludes with a summary of findings and future directions.

## Chapter 2 Background

This chapter reviews the literature that motivates and contextualizes the contributions of this dissertation. We begin by outlining the foundations of apprenticeship learning (AL) and its relationship to reinforcement learning (RL), with particular emphasis on settings where reward functions are difficult to specify and online exploration is infeasible. We then survey advances in inverse reinforcement learning, adversarial imitation learning, and energy-based offline AL that seek to recover robust policies from fixed datasets. Building on this, we review methods addressing heterogeneous and evolving reward functions, imperfect and mixed-quality demonstrations, and contrastive supervision from negative trajectories. Finally, we discuss prior applications of RL and AL to intelligent tutoring systems, which serve as the primary testbed throughout this dissertation. Together, these threads highlight the core methodological challenges—reward ambiguity, distributional shift, and variation in demonstration quality—that this dissertation addresses through a progression of offline apprenticeship learning frameworks.

## 2.1 Sequential Decision-Making under Uncertainty

In many human-centric decision-making environments—such as education and healthcare—an agent must decide which action to take and when to take it as interactions unfold over time. These settings naturally form sequential decision-making problems under uncertainty, where actions influence both immediate and long-term outcomes. Reinforcement Learning (RL) provides a principled framework for modeling such processes by seeking policies that maximize cumulative reward without requiring explicit knowledge of the optimal action at each time step (Sutton and Barto 2018).

Recent advances have demonstrated the effectiveness of RL and Deep RL (DRL) for learning data-driven policies in personalized education, clinical decision support, and adaptive behavioral interventions (Abdelshiheed et al. 2023a; Ju et al. 2021b; Mandel and others 2014; Rowe and Lester 2015). However, several limitations constrain their practical use in real-world human-centric domains. A major challenge is sample inefficiency: DRL algorithms such as Deep Q-Networks (DQN) typically require millions of interactions to learn effective policies, whereas real-world environments often provide only a few thousand trajectories (Ausin et al. 2020b; Shen and Chi 2016). Another challenge arises from reward function design: in domains involving human behavior—such as learning progress or patient well-being—reward signals are difficult to define, non-stationary, and often partially observable (Kaelbling et al. 1996). Hand-crafted reward functions are time-consuming, prone to subjective biases, and may misalign with desired outcomes (Goyal et al. 2019; Abbeel and Ng 2004; Amodei et al. 2016). Moreover, in sensitive human-centric domains, live policy experimentation raises ethical concerns, making it infeasible to collect the exploratory data that RL methods require. These limitations motivate the use of Apprenticeship Learning as an alternative.

## 2.2 Apprenticeship Learning (AL)

Apprenticeship Learning (AL) offers an alternative to reinforcement learning in settings where reward functions are difficult to specify and environment interaction is limited. Rather than relying on hand-crafted rewards or extensive exploration, AL seeks to learn effective policies directly from expert demonstrations by inferring the latent reward structure that best explains expert behavior (Abbeel and Ng 2004). Under the assumption that expert trajectories reflect optimal or near-optimal decision-making, AL methods aim to approximate expert-level performance with far fewer samples than traditional RL (Abbeel and Ng 2004; Ziebart et al. 2008).

Early AL approaches include behavior cloning (BC) (Pomerleau 1991; Syed and Schapire 2012; Raza et al. 2012), which directly maps states to actions by imitating expert behavior. While simple and suitable for purely offline settings, BC suffers from compounding error and poor generalization when demonstrations do not cover the full state space (Ross et al. 2011a). More principled approaches derive from inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) (Abbeel and Ng 2004; Ziebart et al. 2008), which iteratively: (1) infers a reward function from expert data, (2) learns a policy via RL, (3) rolls out the learned policy, and (4) updates reward parameters based on discrepancies from expert trajectories. Although IRL methods are more robust than BC, their reliance on online rollouts limits applicability in human-centric domains where repeated interactions are infeasible or unsafe.

Adversarial imitation learning, exemplified by GAIL (Ho and Ermon 2016b) and AIRL (Fu et al. 2018), advances AL by training a discriminator to distinguish expert from learned behavior and a generator (policy) to minimize this distinction. Off-policy variants (Kostrikov et al. 2018; Kostrikov et al. 2020) reduce the need for online rollouts but inherit adversarial training instability and reliance on off-policy critics, which can introduce bias in offline settings.

These limitations motivated the development of offline AL methods designed to operate on fixed datasets without additional environment interaction. Offline AL must address challenges absent in online settings: distributional shift, the inability to validate policies through rollouts, and reward identifiability without on-policy data. Batch IRL methods (Klein et al. 2011; Lee et al. 2019; Chan and van der Schaar 2021; Raghu et al. 2017b) remove online rollouts but often rely on restrictive reward parameterizations or off-policy evaluation, leading to unstable or suboptimal solutions.

## 2.3 AL with Heterogeneous Reward Functions

To address heterogeneity across demonstrations, several AL methods explicitly model multiple reward functions. Bayesian multi-task IRL (Dimitrakakis and Rothkopf 2011) learns separate reward functions for each trajectory using a shared prior distribution. Choi and Kim (Choi and Kim 2012) extended this using a Dirichlet process mixture to cluster demonstrations without predefining the number of reward types. Arora et al. (Arora et al. 2021) combined Dirichlet processes with maximum entropy IRL to jointly infer clusters and reward functions.

Babes et al. (Babes et al. 2011) proposed an EM-based IRL method that alternates between clustering trajectories and optimizing cluster-specific rewards via maximum-likelihood IRL—a foundational formulation that this dissertation builds upon. Yang et al. applied EM-IRL to student behavior modeling in ITSs (Yang et al. 2020), discovering latent learner subtypes from pedagogical decision data. However, like many multi-intent methods, EM-IRL is constrained to discrete state representations with limited feature sets, and does not generalize to continuous state spaces—a critical limitation in data-rich ITS and healthcare settings with hundreds of continuous features. This dissertation addresses this gap by adapting an EM framework to EDM, enabling heterogeneous reward modeling at scale in continuous spaces.

## 2.4 AL with Evolving Reward Functions

Beyond cross-trajectory heterogeneity, reward functions may also evolve over time within a single trajectory. In tutoring, a student’s goals shift across problem-solving phases; in clinical care, treatment priorities change as a patient’s condition evolves. Single-reward AL and even multi-intent methods fail to capture such intra-trajectory dynamics.

Krishnan et al. introduced Hierarchical IRL (HIRL) (Krishnan et al. 2016), which segments demonstrations into locally linear sub-tasks using Bayesian nonparametric methods and learns sub-task-specific reward functions via maximum entropy IRL. Hausman et al. (Hausman et al. 2017) proposed Multi-modal Imitation Learning (MIL), which jointly discovers sub-tasks and learns corresponding sub-policies using a generative adversarial framework augmented with a latent intention variable. Wang et al. (Wang et al. 2021; Wang et al. 2022) developed hierarchical imitation learning models that simultaneously learn high-level policies and sub-policies for individual sub-tasks. However, these methods commonly rely on online policy rollouts or adversarial training, making them ill-suited for fully offline settings. Meta-imitation learning (Finn et al. 2016) has also been explored for few-shot generalization, but typically requires manually defined sub-task boundaries—impractical in complex, uncertain human-centric environments.

To address these limitations, Yang et al. proposed THEMES (Yang et al. 2025a)—a Time-aware Hierarchical EM Energy-based Subtrajectory AL framework—which automatically partitions trajectories into temporally coherent sub-trajectories using a data-driven method, applies EM-EDM to induce cluster-specific policies over each partition, and refines segmentation through a hierarchically learned reward regulator, all in a strictly offline manner. This dissertation leverages THEMES to model evolving pedagogical and clinical decision-making strategies across both educational and healthcare datasets.

## 2.5 AL from Imperfect and Mixed-Quality Demonstrations

Most AL methods assume access to expert-labeled or at least high-quality demonstrations. In practice, human demonstration corpora are heterogeneous: carefully curated expert trajectories are scarce, while large quantities of suboptimal or partially successful behaviors are readily available. Learning robust policies from mixed-quality data—without inheriting suboptimal biases—is a central challenge.

Early offline approaches based on behavioral cloning perform well with sufficient expert coverage but suffer from compounding error and distributional shift when expert coverage is limited (Ross et al. 2011a). A growing body of work studies learning from imperfect or mixed-quality demonstrations. Confidence-weighted imitation learning (Wu et al. 2019b) incorporates reliability signals to reweight state-action pairs. Discriminator-based reweighting (Xu et al. 2021) learns to down-weight suboptimal data. Positive-unlabeled formulations (Liu et al. 2025) cope with the absence of explicit expert labels. Value- and reward-based approaches such as ORIL (Zolna et al. 2020) and SPRINQL (Hoang et al. 2024) integrate expert and suboptimal experience using offline RL techniques. DemoDICE (Kim et al. 2022a) and NoisyBC (Sasaki and Yamashina 2021b) filter or reweight demonstrations using noise assumptions. ILEED (Beliaev et al. 2022) estimates per-demonstrator expertise scores but requires known demonstrator identities and assumes consistent skill levels within trajectories.

Despite this progress, most mixed-quality AL methods operate in flat policy spaces and apply quality signals at coarse granularity—typically trajectory-level weighting—which can still allow suboptimal decisions to propagate into learned policies. Critically, they do not account for hierarchical or temporally evolving decision-making, where a single trajectory may contain high-quality decisions in some phases and low-quality decisions in others. This dissertation addresses this gap with HALIDE, which integrates continuous demonstration quality signals—derived from Normalized Learning Gain—within a hierarchical policy induction framework, enabling ranked, quality-aware learning while preserving temporal and strategic structure.

## 2.6 Contrastive AL from Negative Demonstrations

A foundational assumption across the imitation learning literature is that failed or negative demonstrations—sequences where outcomes are poor or strategies fail—carry no learnable signal beyond their exclusion. In practice, failed trajectories encode structured information about which strategies fail and why. Contrastive methods aim to exploit this structure.

Prior contrastive approaches to imitation learning use negative demonstrations primarily as contrast signals to improve the discriminability of expert behavior (Tangkaratt et al. 2021). These methods typically apply anti-imitation uniformly across failed trajectories, treating all steps as equally undesirable. This is problematic in hierarchical settings: a failed trajectory often contains segments of strategy-consistent, correct behavior interrupted by a localized divergence that precipitates failure. Applying uniform anti-imitation across such trajectories discards informative correct sub-trajectories and introduces noisy gradient signals.

This dissertation introduces CHALIDE, which addresses this limitation by localizing failure-inducing divergence points within negative trajectories through a novel cluster-assignment divergence statistic derived from learned hierarchical strategy clusters. By applying temporally-weighted anti-imitation only at detected divergence points and enforcing cluster-level reward ordering constraints during hierarchical reward inference, CHALIDE exploits the full informative content of negative trajectories—both the strategy-consistent segments and the failure-inducing transitions—without the distortions of uniform anti-imitation.

## 2.7 RL and AL in Intelligent Tutoring Systems

Intelligent Tutoring Systems (ITSs) provide a primary testbed for sequential decision-making methods, as they require adaptive pedagogical policies that respond to individual student learning states over extended interactions. A large body of prior work has applied RL and DRL to pedagogical policy induction (Mandel and others 2014; Rowe and Lester 2015; Shen and Chi 2016). Shen et al. (Shen and Chi 2016) applied value iteration to induce policies targeting student performance improvement. Zhou et al. (Zhou et al. 2019) employed offline hierarchical RL to derive pedagogical policies, achieving superior results over flat RL baselines in classroom studies. Abdelshiheed et al. (Abdelshiheed et al. 2023b) demonstrated that deep RL-based metacognitive interventions can close skill gaps between student subgroups and support preparation for future learning.

These RL approaches typically require large-scale training data and rely on predefined reward functions such as normalized learning gain, limiting adaptability to diverse student populations (Shen and Chi 2016; Zhou et al. 2019). Conservative Q-Learning (CQL) (Kumar et al. 2020) has been applied to ITSs to address overestimation bias in offline settings, most notably as a student-choice-with-override policy (Hostetter et al. 2023b) in which the system intervenes only when its recommendation disagrees with the student’s autonomous choice.

AL has been explored as an alternative to reward-based RL in ITS settings. Rafferty et al. (Rafferty et al. 2015) applied IRL to infer learners’ beliefs in an educational game, demonstrating IRL’s potential for interpreting interactive learning data. Yang et al. (Yang et al. 2020) applied EM-IRL to cluster students by pedagogical behavior, though limited to discrete state representations. Islam et al. (Islam et al. 2024) extended this with EM-EDM, enabling policy induction from heterogeneous expert demonstrations in large continuous state spaces and demonstrating that AL outperforms two DRL and four AL baselines on pedagogical action prediction. This dissertation extends this line of work, culminating in the first live classroom deployment of an EDM-induced student-choice-with-override policy (StuEDM), which outperforms CQL trained on a large historical corpus across multiple normalized learning gain measures without additional study time.

## Chapter 3 Modeling Heterogeneous Student Pedagogical Strategies

* This chapter has been published as:

Md Mirajul Islam, Xi Yang, John Hostetter, Adittya Soukarjya Saha, and Min Chi. “A Generalized Apprenticeship Learning Framework for Modeling Heterogeneous Student Pedagogical Strategies.” Proceedings of the 17th International Conference on Educational Data Mining (EDM 2024).

A key challenge in e-learning environments like Intelligent Tutoring Systems (ITSs) is to induce effective pedagogical policies efficiently. While Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL) often suffers from sample inefficiency and reward function design difficulty, Apprenticeship Learning (AL) algorithms can overcome them. However, most AL algorithms can not handle heterogeneity as they assume all demonstrations are generated with a homogeneous policy driven by a single reward function. Still, some AL algorithms which consider heterogeneity, often can not generalize to large continuous state space and only work with discrete states. In this paper, we propose an expectation-maximization(EM)-EDM, a general AL framework to induce effective pedagogical policies from given optimal or near-optimal demonstrations, which are assumed to be driven by heterogeneous reward functions. We compare the effectiveness of the policies induced by our proposed EM-EDM against four AL-based baselines and two policies induced by DRL on two different but related tasks that involve pedagogical action prediction. Our overall results showed that, for both tasks, EM-EDM outperforms the four AL baselines across all performance metrics and the two DRL baselines. This suggests that EM-EDM can effectively model complex student pedagogical decision-making processes through the ability to manage a large, continuous state space and adapt to handle diverse and heterogeneous reward functions with very few given demonstrations.

## 3.1 Overview

Reinforcement Learning (RL) and Deep RL (DRL) have experienced significant development in recent years and have been successfully applied in e-learning systems such as intelligent tutoring systems (ITSs) (Abdelshiheed et al. 2023a; Chi et al. 2011b; Chi et al. 2011a; Hostetter et al. 2023a; Iglesias et al. 2009; Shen and Chi 2016). More specifically, in an ITS, the pedagogical agent leverages RL to learn a decision-making policy to maximize the expected cumulative rewards over an extended period, with the overarching aim of benefiting students in the long run (Abdelshiheed et al. 2023b; Hostetter et al. 2023b; Ju et al. 2022). Despite DRL’s great success, several obstacles prevent the broader application of DRL to educational systems in practice. One is sample inefficiency. For example, it takes Deep Q-Networks (DQN), one type of classic DRL algorithm, hundreds of millions of interactions with the environment to learn a good policy and generalize to unseen states.
In many previous studies on RL and DRL in ITSs, a prevalent practice involves generating an exploratory corpus. This is typically done by training a cohort of students on an ITS that produces random, yet reasonable, decisions. Subsequently, RL is applied to derive pedagogical policies from this training corpus. However, it is noteworthy that despite this approach, a significant portion of prior research aims to derive pedagogical policies from fewer than 3000 logs of student-tutor interactions; the resultant RL policies may sometimes be proven ineffective (Ausin et al. 2020b; Shen and Chi 2016).

Another major obstacle is reward function design. As a fundamental element in RL, the reward function defines the goals to achieve when the agent interacts with the environment, which provides the agent incentive to adopt better decision-making behaviors (Kaelbling et al. 1996). Specifically, the reward function maps each perceived state (or state-action pair) of the environment to a scalar value and gives it as a credit or punishment to the RL agent, and the agent will learn a decision-making policy accordingly to maximize the expected cumulative rewards. Just as supervised models depend heavily on accurate labels for the training dataset, the effectiveness and robustness of RL approaches depend heavily on an accurate reward function.
Despite the importance of the reward function in RL, it is usually difficult to design, especially for human-centric tasks like education, where multiple factors need to be covered and traded off. Generally, the reward function is hand-crafted beforehand, separate from the policy induction. However, manually designing an appropriate reward function is always labor-intensive and time-consuming (Goyal et al. 2019). It commonly depends on the domain knowledge, and it is hard to avoid the expertise blind spots (Abbeel and Ng 2004). Moreover, the manually specified reward function will likely be miss-specified, which can be inconsistent with the expected policy (Amodei et al. 2016).

To overcome the two obstacles, Apprenticeship Learning (AL) algorithms have been proposed. Instead of inducing the policy under the guidance of a pre-defined reward function and using a large amount of training data, AL aims at learning via observing and imitating a few demonstrations provided by expert agents, who make decisions optimally or near-optimally with respect to an unknown underlying reward function (Abbeel and Ng 2004). By learning from demonstrations, the learner agent, i.e., the apprentice, aims to learn a decision-making policy to behave as well as the experts. Getting rid of the difficulties in reward function design in RL, AL has been widely applied in various applications (Asoh et al. 2013; Hostetter et al. 2023c; Pan et al. 2019; Rafferty et al. 2015; Wang et al. 2020).

AL approaches have been categorized as being either online or offline. In the former category, the agent learns while interacting with the environment; the latter learns the policy from pre-collected data.
The existing AL are commonly online, requiring interacting with the environment iteratively for collecting new data and then updating the model accordingly (Abbeel and Ng 2004; Ziebart et al. 2008; Finn et al. 2016). Online AL algorithms are generally appropriate for domains where interacting with simulations and actual environments is computationally cheap and feasible. On the other hand, for domains such as e-learning, building accurate simulations or simulated students is incredibly challenging because human learning is a rather complex, poorly understood process; moreover, learning policies while interacting with students can not only be unethical but not allowed. Therefore, we focus on offline AL approaches. A recently proposed energy-based distribution matching (EDM) approach (Jarrett et al. 2020a) has advanced the state-of-the-art in offline AL. However, EDM assumes all demonstrations are generated using a homogeneous policy with a single reward function. In real-world scenarios, the reward function can be heterogeneous across the demonstrations. For example, in the education domain, when students make decisions during learning, their reward functions can be learning-oriented, efficient-oriented, or not learning (Yang et al. 2020). Therefore, in this work, to handle the heterogeneous reward functions, we propose an expectation-maximization(EM)-EDM, a general AL framework to induce pedagogical policies from given optimal or near-optimal demonstrations, which are assumed to be driven by multiple heterogeneous reward functions.

While EM-Inverse Reinforcement Learning (EM-IRL) was introduced in (Yang et al. 2020), it is limited because it can only handle relatively small discrete state representation (17 discrete features involved in (Yang et al. 2020)). In contrast, our proposed EM-EDM can handle over 140 continuous features.
In this study, we focused on the decisions on whether to present the next problem as a Worked Example (WE), a Problem Solving (PS), or a Faded Worked Example (FWE). In WE, students were given a detailed example showing how the tutor solves a problem; in PS, by contrast, students were tasked with solving the same problem on their own on the ITS; in FWEs, the students and the tutor co-construct in that their solutions are intertwined.

In AL, it is usually assumed that the demonstrations are optimally or near optimally executed by experts (Abbeel and Ng 2004). Thus, the quality of the demonstrations matters in order to induce more effective policies. In this study, rather than gathering exploratory data in DRL, our EM-EDM will derive pedagogical policies directly from the pedagogical decisions and behaviors demonstrated by students while interacting with the ITS. In this study, we involve 53 “optimal” or “near-optimal” demonstrations, which empower students to make their own pedagogical decisions during learning, leading to positive learning outcomes (see details in Section 3.4.3).

We compare the effectiveness of the policies induced by our proposed EM-EDM against four AL-based baselines using the 53 demonstrations and two policies induced by DRL using 2,716 and 1,819 student-ITSs interactive trajectories, respectively. This evaluation is conducted on two tasks that are related but distinct from each other. For Task 1, we adhere to standard AL research practices by conducting training and evaluation of the methods for pedagogical action prediction. This is accomplished through 5-fold cross-validation, utilizing all selected demonstrations.
Task 2 holds greater relevance and practical significance in the educational data mining field. It involves using trajectories from a previous semester as training data and trajectories from a subsequent semester as testing data. The objective is to assess whether the clustering results obtained from the training data of the previous semester can effectively predict students’ actions in the later semester. Our overall results showed that, for both tasks, EM-EDM outperforms the four AL baselines across all performance metrics as well as the two DRL baselines. This suggests that EM-EDM is more data efficient and effective in learning students’ heterogeneous strategies in ITSs. Beyond this educational setting, we additionally ask whether the heterogeneous-reward principle behind EM-EDM generalizes to a second, higher-stakes human-centric domain: we validate EM-EDM against its two core baselines—behavior cloning (BC) and EDM’s own single-reward assumption—on three clinical EHR datasets for septic shock prevention.

In summary, our contributions can be outlined as follows:

1. 

The key contribution lies in the capability of EM-EDM to effectively model complex student pedagogical decision-making processes through the ability to manage a large, continuous state space and its adaptability in handling diverse and heterogeneous reward functions.

2. 

The empirical evaluation of EM-EDM involves comparing four state-of-the-art AL baselines and two DRL baselines on two significant tasks.

3. 

The results obtained from EM-EDM showed that students’ demonstrations exhibit heterogeneous reward functions. Notably, EM-EDM can induce distinct pedagogical policies for different reward functions even with as few as 24 demonstrations.

4. 

We validate EM-EDM beyond education on three clinical EHR datasets for septic shock prevention, showing that modeling heterogeneous reward functions is not specific to student pedagogical decisions but generalizes to clinical decision-making as well.

## 3.2 Related Work

## 3.2.1 RL and DRL for Pedagogical Policy Induction

In general, there have been two types of prior research on using Reinforcement Learning (RL) to educational policy induction: classic RL and DRL approaches. With the ultimate goal of helping students in the long run, the pedagogical agent in an ITS uses RL to learn a decision-making policy that maximizes the predicted cumulative benefits over a prolonged period (Ju et al. 2022). Despite DRL’s enormous success, several challenges are preventing its wider implementation in actual educational institutions; sample inefficiency is one. For instance, learning a suitable policy and generalizing to unknown states requires hundreds of millions of interactions with the environment for Deep Q-Networks (DQN), one kind of classic DRL method.

The design of reward functions is another significant barrier. They are a key component of reinforcement learning and establish objectives for the agent to meet when interacting with the environment. This motivates the agent to make better decisions (Kaelbling et al. 1996). In particular, the reward function assigns a credit or punishment to each perceived state (or state-action combination) of the environment. Based on this, the RL agent learns a decision-making policy that maximizes the expected cumulative rewards. An accurate reward function is critical to the efficacy and robustness of reinforcement learning techniques, much as supervised models rely significantly on accurate labels for the training dataset.

Even though the reward function is crucial to reinforcement learning, designing one is typically challenging, particularly for tasks that focus on people, like teaching, and require balancing various considerations. Usually, the policy induction is done separately from the reward function, which is manually created previously. However, creating a suitable reward function by hand is usually time-consuming and labor-intensive (Goyal et al. 2019).

To summarize, previous research indicates that RL and DRL-induced pedagogical policies can be sample inefficient and require handling the challenge of reward function design.

## 3.2.2 Apprenticeship Learning (AL)

Behavior cloning (Syed and Schapire 2012; Raza et al. 2012) is a traditional offline AL technique that learns a mapping from states to actions by avariciously copying the best practices of experts who have been shown (Pomerleau 1991). Several inverse reinforcement learning (IRL)-based (Abbeel and Ng 2004; Ziebart et al. 2008) and adversarial imitation learning-based (Ho and Ermon 2016b) techniques have been developed to better capture the data distribution in experts’ presentations.

In general, iterative loops are used in IRL-based approaches to 1) infer a reward function, 2) induce a policy using traditional reinforcement learning, 3) roll out the learned policy, and 4) update the reward parameters in response to discrepancies between the roll-out behaviors and expert demonstrations. The technical and theoretical drawbacks of the original IRL approaches that adhere to the aforementioned online model will be retained when applied to an offline environment. Furthermore, the reward is typically modeled by IRL-based approaches using a certain tractable format, such as a linear function that maps states and state-action pairings to reward values (Abbeel and Ng 2004; Ziebart et al. 2008; Babes et al. 2011). Moreover, certain batch-IRL have been proposed in order to prevent the learned policy from being implemented.

Typically, adversarial imitation learning-based techniques involve learning a discriminator to discern learned behaviors from expert demonstrations and a generator to implement the policy iteratively. Some off-policy learning techniques based on off-policy actor-critic have been developed under the batch setting in order to avoid rolling out the policy (Kostrikov et al. 2018; Kostrikov et al. 2020). However, according to (Ho and Ermon 2016b), these techniques inherit the complicated alternating max-min optimization from general adversarial imitation learning.

Jarrett et al. proposed and evaluated the EDM (Jarrett et al. 2020a) on a wide range of benchmarks, online environments like e.g., Acrobot, LunarLander, and BeamRider, as well as offline environment MIMIC-III. It was shown that EDM can outperform both IRL-based and adversarial imitation learning-based methods. In this work, EDM serves as one of our baselines.

## 3.2.3 AL with Multiple Intentions

A few AL methods have been proposed to consider multiple reward functions. A Bayesian multi-task IRL was proposed, which models the heterogeneity of reward functions by formalizing it as a statistical preference elicitation via a joint reward-policy prior (Dimitrakakis and Rothkopf 2011). Choi and Kim integrated a Dirichlet process mixture model into Bayesian IRL to cluster the demonstrations (Choi and Kim 2012). Using a Bayesian model, they incorporated the domain knowledge of multiple reward functions. Similarly, Arora et al. combined the Dirichlet process with a maximum entropy IRL to learn the clusters of demonstration with different reward functions (Arora et al. 2021).

Babes et al. derived an EM-based IRL approach that clusters trajectories based on their different reward functions (Babes et al. 2011). As a component of the EM-based IRL, a maximum-likelihood IRL uses a gradient ascent method to optimize the reward parameters, which has successfully identified unknown reward functions. Xi et al. used EM-Inverse Reinforcement Learning (EM-IRL) to subtype students from their pedagogical behavior data (Yang et al. 2020). Still, as most other multiple intentions works, it is limited because it can only handle discrete state representation (17 discrete features involved in (Yang et al. 2020)) and can not generalize to large continuous state space.

## 3.2.4 WE, PS, and FWE

Many studies have examined the effectiveness of WE, PS, and FWE, as well as their different combinations (Van Gog et al. 2011; Renkl et al. 2002; Najar et al. 2014).
Renkl et al. (Renkl et al. 2002) compared WE-FWE-PS with WE-PS pairs, and the results showed that the WE-FWE-PS condition significantly outperformed the WE-PS condition on posttest scores. Similarly, Najar et al. (Najar et al. 2014) compared adaptive WE/FWE/PS with WE-PS pairs and found that the former is significantly more effective than the latter on improving student learning.
Overall, it is demonstrated that adaptively alternating amongst WE, PS, and FWE is more effective than hand-coded expert rules in improving student learning.
However, when students make decisions among WE, PS, and FWE, there’s no significant difference with tutors making decisions on students’ learning performance (Zhou and Chi 2017). As far as we know, no prior research has explored how to combine students’ decision-making with RL-induced policy decision-making to facilitate learning.

## 3.3 Methodology

AL generally follows the procedure of 1) taking the demonstrations as input to infer the latent reward function, based on which a policy can be induced, then 2) rolling out the learned policy to update the reward function by minimizing the divergence between the rolled-out behaviors versus the demonstrations. This procedure will be conducted iteratively until convergence.

The input of EM-EDM contains $N$ demonstrated trajectories $\{\xi^{n}\}=\{(\mathbf{s}_{t}^{n},a_{t}^{n})|t=1,...,T^{n};n=1,...,N\}$ provided by experts, where $\mathbf{s}_{t}^{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}$ is the $t$ -th multivariate state with $m$ features, and $a_{t}^{n}$ is the corresponding action in the $n$ -th trajectory with the length of $T^{n}$ . In this work, we assume that $\{\xi^{n}\}$ are carried out with multiple policies. EM-EDM would cluster the trajectories first and then induce policies for each cluster to induce effective policies. Taking our ITSs as an example, EM-EDM will cluster student trajectories and induce different policies or strategies over learned clusters (as shown in Figure 3.1).

Algorithm 1 EM-EDM.

 1:

  Input: Expert trajectories $\{\xi^{n}\}$ , number of clusters $\{K\}$ 

 2:

  Initialize: Prior probability $\nu_{j}$ and policy parameter $\theta_{j}$ , $j=1,...K$ randomly

 3:

  repeat

 4:

   E Step: Compute the $u_{ij}$ , $i=1,...N$ , $j=1,...K$ 

 5:

   M Step: Update $\nu_{j}$ and learn $\theta_{j}$ via EDM, $j=1,...K$ 

 6:

  until stop_criteria is True

 7:

  Output: Cluster-wise policies $\{\Pi^{\theta}_{j}|j=1,...K\}$ 

## 3.3.1 EDM

As a strictly offline AL, energy-based distribution matching (EDM) (Jarrett et al. 2020a) can learn the policy merely based on the experts’ demonstrations, not requiring any knowledge of model transitions or off-policy evaluations. It assumes that the demonstrations $\{\xi^{n}\}$ are carried out with a policy $\Pi^{\theta}$ parameterized by $\theta$ , driven by a single reward function.

We denote the state-action pair as $(\mathbf{s},a)$ . The occupancy measures for the demonstrations and the learned policy are denoted as $\rho_{\xi}$ and $\rho_{\Pi^{\theta}}$ , respectively.
The probability density for each state-action pair can be measured as: $\rho_{\Pi^{\theta}}(\mathbf{s},a)=\mathbb{E}_{\Pi^{\theta}}[\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\mathds{1}{\{\mathbf{s}_{t}=\mathbf{s},a_{t}=a\}}]$ , where $\gamma$ is a discount factor, then the probability density for each state can be measured by: $\rho_{\Pi^{\theta}}(\mathbf{s})=\sum_{a}\rho_{\Pi^{\theta}}(\mathbf{s},a)$ . To induce the policy $\Pi^{\theta}$ , our goal is minimizing the KL divergence between $\rho_{\xi}$ and $\rho_{\theta}$ :

 $\argmin_{\theta}D_{KL}(\rho_{\xi}||\rho_{\theta})=\argmin_{\theta}-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{s},a\sim\rho_{\xi}}\log\rho_{\theta}(\mathbf{s},a)$ 

(3.1)

Since $\Pi^{\theta}(a|\mathbf{s})=\rho_{\Pi^{\theta}}(\mathbf{s},a)/\rho_{\Pi^{\theta}}(\mathbf{s})$ , we can formulate the objective function as:

 $\argmin_{\theta}-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{s}\sim\rho_{\xi}}\log\rho_{\Pi^{\theta}}(\mathbf{s})-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{s},a\sim\rho_{\xi}}\log\Pi^{\theta}(a|\mathbf{s})$ 

(3.2)

When there is no access to roll out the policy $\Pi^{\theta}$ in an online manner, $\rho_{\Pi^{\theta}}(\mathbf{s})$ in the first term of Eq.(3.2) would be challenging to estimate. EDM can handle this issue by utilizing an energy-based model (Grathwohl et al. 2019).

According to energy-based model, the probability density $\rho_{\Pi^{\theta}}(\mathbf{s})\propto e^{-E(\mathbf{s})}$ , with $E(\mathbf{s})$ being an energy function.
Then the occupancy measure for state-action pairs can be represented as: $\rho_{\Pi^{\theta}}(\mathbf{s},a)=e^{f_{\Pi^{\theta}}(\mathbf{s})[a]}/Z_{\Pi^{\theta}}$ , and the occupancy measure for states can be obtained by marginalizing out the actions: $\rho_{\Pi^{\theta}}(\mathbf{s})=\sum_{a}e^{f_{\Pi^{\theta}}(\mathbf{s})[a]}/Z_{\Pi^{\theta}}$ . Herein, $Z_{\Pi^{\theta}}$ is a partition function, and $f_{\Pi^{\theta}}:\mathbb{R}^{|S|}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{|A|}}$ is a parametric function that mapping each state to $A$ real-valued numbers.

The parameterization of $\Pi^{\theta}$ implicitly defines an energy-based model over the state’s distribution, where the energy function can be defined as: $E_{\Pi^{\theta}}(\mathbf{s})=-\log\sum_{a}e^{f_{\Pi\theta}(\mathbf{s})[a]}$ .
Under the scope of the energy-based model, the first term in Eq.(3.2) can be reformulated as an “occupancy” loss:

 $\mathcal{L}_{\rho}(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{s}\sim\rho_{\xi}}E_{\theta}(\mathbf{s})-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{s}\sim\rho_{\Pi^{\theta}}}E_{\theta}(\mathbf{s})$ 

(3.3)

where $\nabla_{\theta}\mathcal{L}_{\rho}(\theta)=-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{s}\sim\rho_{\xi}}\nabla_{\theta}\log\rho_{\Pi^{\theta}}(\mathbf{s})$ can be solved by existing optimizers, e.g., stochastic gradient Langevin dynamics (Welling and Teh 2011). Therefore, by substituting the first term in Eq.(3.2) as Eq.(3.3) via energy-based model, we can derive a surrogate objective function to get the optimal solution without the need of online rolling out the policy.

## 3.3.2 EM-EDM

To deal with multiple reward functions varying across the demonstrations, Babes-Vroman et al. proposed an EM-based inverse reinforcement learning (Babes et al. 2011) by iteratively clustering the demonstrations in E-step and inducing policies for each cluster by IRL in M-step. Specifically, in the M-step, they explored several IRL methods based on the discrete states, which is not scalable for large continuous state spaces, e.g., ITSs logs.
Enlightened by this EM framework and the success of EDM, we proposed an EM-EDM (Algorithm 1) (Yang et al. 2023a).

Figure 3.1: EM-EDM framework.

Taking trajectories $\{\xi^{i}|i=1,...,N\}$ as input, with $N$ being the number of trajectories, EM-EDM aims to cluster these trajectories and learn the cluster-wise policies $\{\Pi_{j}|j=1,...K\}$ , where $K$ is the number of clusters. $\nu_{j}$ and $\theta_{j}$ , denote the prior probability and the policy parameter for each cluster, and both are randomly initialized.
The objective function of EM-EDM is to maximize the log-likelihood defined in Eq.(3.4):

 $\argmax_{\theta_{j}}\underset{j=1}{\overset{K}{\sum}}\underset{i=1}{\overset{N}{\sum}}log(u_{ij})$ 

(3.4)

where $u_{ij}$ denotes the probability that trajectory $\xi^{i}$ follows the policy of the $j$ -th cluster. It is defined in Eq.(3.5), with $U$ being a normalization factor.

 $u_{ij}=Pr(\xi^{i}|\theta_{j})=\underset{(\mathbf{s},a)\in\xi^{i}}{\prod}\frac{\Pi_{\theta_{j}}(\mathbf{s},a)\nu_{j}}{U}$ 

(3.5)

During the EM process, in the E-step, the probability that trajectory $\xi^{i}$ belonging to cluster $j$ is calculated by Eq.(3.5).
Then in the M-step, the prior probabilities are updated via $\nu_{j}=\sum_{i}u_{ij}/N$ , and the policy parameters $\theta_{j}$ is learned by EDM. The E-step and M-step are iteratively executed until converged. Finally, the output of EM-EDM is the clustered trajectories with their respective policies.

## 3.3.3 Apprenticeship Learning Baselines

Behavior cloning (BC)

Behavior cloning (Syed and Schapire 2012; Raza et al. 2012) is a traditional offline AL technique that learns a mapping from states to actions by avariciously copying the best practices of experts who have been shown (Pomerleau 1991). It is a supervised learning approach used in AL where an agent learns to imitate an expert by directly mapping observations to actions based on the expert’s demonstrations. The objective is to mimic the expert’s behavior. The learning process involves minimizing the difference between the agent’s actions and those demonstrated by the expert (Bain and Sommut 1999).

In a simplified form, let’s consider a dataset of expert demonstrations where each example consists of an observation-action pair: $(s_{i},a_{i})$ , where $s_{i}$ is an observation and $a_{i}$ is the corresponding action. The behavior cloning objective is to learn a policy $\pi_{\theta}(a|s)$ parameterized by $\theta$ that approximates the expert’s behavior.

The loss function for behavior cloning can be defined as the standard supervised learning loss:

 $L(\theta)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lVert\pi_{\theta}(a_{i}|s_{i})-a_{i}\rVert^{2}$ 

(3.6)

Where $N$ is the length of trajectories. The goal is to minimize this loss, and the optimal $\theta$ corresponds to a policy that imitates the expert’s actions.

It’s important to note that while BC is straightforward, it can be sensitive to distribution shifts and compounding errors, especially when the learned policy deviates from the expert’s demonstrations.

Generative Adversarial Imitation Learning (GAIL)

Generative Adversarial Imitation Learning (GAIL) is another AL approach that uses a generative adversarial network (GAN) (Goodfellow et al. 2014) to model the reward function, making the learned policy less sensitive to differences between the demonstrator and the learner (Ho and Ermon 2016a). GAIL aims to generate a reward function that encourages the learner’s policy to match the demonstrated behavior.

Given a set of expert trajectories $\tau_{E}\sim\pi_{E}$ , and initial policy and discriminator parameter to be $\theta_{0},w_{0}$ , GAIL iteratively updates the discriminator parameters from $w_{i}$ to $w_{i+1}$ with the gradient as per Equation 3.7.

 $\hat{E}_{\tau_{i}}\left[\nabla_{w}\log(D_{w}(s,a))\right]+\hat{E}_{\tau_{E}}\left[\nabla_{w}\log(1-D_{w}(s,a))\right]$ 

(3.7)

And then, take a KL-constrained natural gradient step with Equation 3.8.

 $\begin{split}\hat{E}_{\tau_{i}}\left[\nabla_{\theta}\log\pi_{\theta}(a|s)Q(s,a)\right]-\lambda\nabla_{\theta}H(\pi_{\theta}),\\
\text{ where }Q(\bar{s},\bar{a})=\hat{E}_{\tau_{i}}\left[\log(D_{w_{i+1}}(s,a))\,|\,s_{0}=\bar{s},a_{0}=\bar{a}\right]\end{split}$ 

(3.8)

Where $\pi_{\theta}$ is the policy of the learner, and $\pi_{\text{E}}$ is the expert policy. The discriminator aims to maximize this objective, while the learner’s policy minimizes it. The reward function is derived from the discriminator’s output.

GAIL addresses distribution shift issues in AL and is more robust than behavior cloning. However, it may face challenges such as mode collapse during GAN training.

Adversarial Inverse Reinforcement Learning (AIRL)

Adversarial Inverse Reinforcement Learning (AIRL) is an adversarial approach in AL similar to GAIL that adversarially trains a policy against a discriminator that aims to distinguish the expert demonstrations from the learned policy. Unlike GAIL, AIRL recovers a reward function more generalizable to changes in environment dynamics (Fu et al. 2018).

Given a set of expert demonstrations: $\tau_{i}^{E}$ , initial policy $\pi$ and discriminator $D_{\theta,\phi}$ , AIRL iteratively collect trajectories $\tau_{i}$ by executing $\pi$ , train the discriminator via binary logistic regression to classify expert data from samples. The reward is updated in each step following the Equation 3.9. Later, the policy is updated with respect to reward using any policy optimization method.

 $r_{\theta,\phi}(s,a,s^{\prime})=\log D_{\theta,\phi}(s,a,s^{\prime})-\log(1-D_{\theta,\phi}(s,a,s^{\prime}))$ 

(3.9)

where $\pi_{\theta}$ is the policy of the learner, and $\pi_{\text{expert}}$ is the expert policy. Like GAIL, the discriminator aims to maximize this objective while the learner’s policy minimizes it. The reward function is derived from the discriminator’s output.

AIRL offers advantages such as handling distribution shifts and being less sensitive to suboptimal demonstrations. However, it may be computationally expensive.

## 3.3.4 Two Deep RL Baselines : CQL & CQL-T

CQL

A good number of prior research has explored the Deep RL (DRL) (e.g., (Ju et al. 2021b; Mandel and others 2014; Rowe and Lester 2015)), and it has shown that they can be used to induce effective pedagogical policies for ITSs (Mandel and others 2014; Wang et al. 2017). As a DRL baseline, we choose Conservative Q-Learning (CQL) (Kumar et al. 2020), known for addressing overestimation issues in comparison to various existing RL methods, including Deep Q-learning, which is extensively explored in prior DRL studies within ITSs.

CQL represents a Q-learning or actor-critic algorithm designed to learn Q-functions, ensuring that the anticipated policy value under the learned Q-function provides a conservative estimate of the actual policy value. To achieve Q-values with lower bounds, CQL goes beyond standard minimization by concurrently minimizing the Q-function under a selected distribution and maximizing it under the data distribution (Kumar et al. 2020). The training objective for the Q-function is accordingly structured to attain this goal:

 $\displaystyle\hat{Q}_{\mathrm{CQL}}^{\pi}:=\arg\min_{Q}\alpha\cdot(\underbrace{E_{\mathbf{s}\sim\mathcal{D},\mathbf{a}\sim\mu(\mathbf{a}\mid\mathbf{s})}[Q(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})]}_{\text{minimize Q-values }}$ 

(3.10)

 $\displaystyle-\underbrace{E_{\mathbf{s}\sim\mathcal{D},\mathbf{a}\sim\hat{\pi}_{\beta}(\mathbf{a}\mid\mathbf{s})}[Q(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})]}_{\text{maximize Q-values under data }})$ 

 $\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\underbrace{E_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a},\mathbf{s}^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(Q(s,a)-\left(\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{s},a)+\gamma\left[\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}Q\left(\mathbf{s}^{\prime},a\right)\right]\right)^{2}\right]\right.}_{\text{standard Bellman error }}$ 

Then, the optimization of the policy is done w.r.t: 

 $\displaystyle\hat{Q}_{\mathrm{CQL}}^{\pi}:\pi\leftarrow\arg\max_{\pi}E_{\pi}\left[\hat{Q}_{\mathrm{CQL}}^{\pi}\right]$ 

(3.11)

The offline training process continues until it hits convergence, a predetermined error threshold, or the maximum number of iterations. As the amount of accessible data increases, the magnitude of $\alpha$ may decrease, so over here, $\alpha$ > 0 represents a trade-off factor (Kumar et al. 2020). Here, $\hat{\pi}_{\beta}$ denotes the behavior policy,
 $\gamma\in[0,1]$ denotes the discount factor, $\mathbf{s}$ is the current state, $\mathbf{s}^{\prime}$ is the next state and $\mathscr{R}$ corresponds to the reward function.

CQL-T

CQL-T refers to the same methodology for policy induction as CQL except that it utilizes a combined reward function of both student training time and Normalized Learning Gain (NLG) motivated by prior research that response time reveals student proficiency (Schnipke and Scrams 2002). In particular, there was a significant negative correlation between student average response time and student final exam score taken at the end of the semester (González-Espada and Bullock 2007).

## 3.4 Experiments

We gathered our data by letting undergraduate students from the same major and same year complete an online intelligent tutoring system (ITS) (Figure 3.2) that introduced them to probability concepts like Bayes’ Theorem and Addition Theorem. Using training problems, the students were guided through the instruction. The tutor gave detailed instructions, quick responses, and on-demand assistance for every problem. The assistance was given in the form of progressively more detailed hints. The bottom-out hint, the final one in the sequence, gave the students precise instructions. Students could decide throughout training how to solve the next stage pedagogically by either working through it alone, watching the tutor work through it, or doing it collaboratively. The tutor will ask questions to get the answer if they want to solve it independently; if not, the instructor will show or tell.

Figure 3.2: The interface of our probability tutor.

## 3.4.1 Data Collection

Our experiment involves 128 students from two semesters, Spring 21 (67 students) and Spring 22 (61 students). The textbook, pre-test, training, and post-test were the four stages every student taking part in our data collection process went through. All students studied the domain fundamentals from a textbook on probability during the textbook. They studied examples of each principle, read a general explanation, and worked through various single- and multi-principle problems. After that, the students completed a pretest with 8 problems. They would not receive feedback on their responses during this phase and would not be permitted to revisit previous questions (this also applied to the post-test). During training, twelve problems are shown in a fixed sequence, allowing students to practice by choosing to solve it alone (PS), solving it collaboratively with the ITS (FWE), or seeing the solution as a worked-out example (WE). Students work through the problem step-by-step, define variables, type equations, etc. The fewest steps required to complete each training problem varied between 20 and 50. While making decisions on how to solve the next problem in training, if PS is chosen, the ITS asks questions to elicit the next step’s answer from the student; if FWE is chosen, the ITS chooses to elicit or tell the answer; if WE is chosen, the ITS tells the answer. There were between three and eleven domain principles needed to address each problem. At last, every student completed the post-test, which comprised 12 questions. Four problems were more complex, non-isomorphic multiple-principle problems, and the remaining 8 were isomorphic to the problems provided in the pre-test phase.

Students had to write and solve one or more equations to arrive at an answer for the pre and post-tests. Three scoring criteria were applied: binary, partial credit, and one point for each premise. A solution received one point under the binary rubric for being entirely correct and zero points for being incorrect. The percentage of correctly applied principles seen in the solution determined each problem’s score under the partial credit rubric. For example, learners would receive a 0.8 score if they correctly implemented 4 of the 5 potential principles. A point was awarded under the one-point-per-principle rubric for every correctly applied principle. Each student’s answers to all the pre-test and post-test problems were double-blind, graded by 2 experienced graders, and then aggregated into one final grade by resolving disagreements if there were any. All test scores were normalized to the range of [0 - 100] for comparison purposes.

Moreover, all students’ data were obtained anonymously through an exempt IRB-approved protocol, and we scored them using double-blinded grading rubrics. All data were de-identified, and no demographic data or grades were collected. This research seeks to remove societal harms from lower engagement and retention of students who need more personalized interventions for introductory Computer Science Courses.

## 3.4.2 States & Actions

Continuous State Space

142 continuous state features were extracted from the student system interaction log data. Here is a brief description of the features:

 $\bullet$ Autonomy(10 features): the amount of work done by a student, such as the number of elicits since the last tell;

 $\bullet$ Temporal (29 features): time-related information about the student’s behavior, such as the average time per step;

 $\bullet$ Problem Solving (35 features): information about the current problem-solving context, such as problem difficulty;

 $\bullet$ Performance (57 features): information about the student’s performance so far, such as the percentage of correct entries;

 $\bullet$ Hints (11 features): information about the student’s hint usage, such as the total number of hints requested.

Pedagogical Action Space

The students can take three decisions which can be interpreted as actions which are elicit(PS), tell(WE) or collaboration(FWE), i.e., to elicit the solution by themselves through asking questions, to let the tutor tell them the solution directly or to work with the tutor collaboratively towards the solution respectively.

## 3.4.3 Selecting Demonstrations (A total of 53)

To enhance the selection of high-quality demonstrations for inducing more accurate yet effective AL policies, it is typically believed that the experts are performing the demonstrations in an optimal or near-optimal manner (Abbeel and Ng 2004).
Our original dataset contains 128 students from two semesters, Spring 21 (67 students) and Spring 22 (61 students). Each student spent 2̃ hours on the probability ITS and completed around 400 steps.
To select higher-quality trajectories from all students’ interaction with the ITS, we use a qualitative measurement called Quantized Learning Gain (QLG) (Lin and Chi 2017), which is a binary qualitative measurement of students’ learning gains from the pretest to the posttest to determine whether a student has benefited from a learning environment (Mao et al. 2018).

Figure 3.3: Quantized Learning Gain.

To calculate QLG, first, students were split into low, medium, and high-performance groups based on whether they scored below the 33rd percentile, between the 33rd and 66th percentile, or higher than the 66th percentile in pre-test and post-test, respectively. Once a student’s pre-test and post-test performance groups (high, medium, or low) are decided, the student is a “High” QLG if he/she moved from a lower performance group to a higher performance group from pre-test to post-test or remained in “high” performance groups; whereas a “low” QLG is assigned to the student if he/she either moved from a higher performance group to a lower performance group from pre-test to post-test or stayed at a “low” or “medium” groups (as shown in Figure 3.3). In Figure 3.3, solid lines represent the formation of the High QLG groups, and dashed lines represent the formation of the Low QLG groups. Following this procedure, Table 3.1 illustrates the number of trajectories in each category across the two semesters. This procedure resulted in a total of 53 “high-quality” student trajectories, which are treated as optimal or near-optimal demonstrations in the following analysis.

Table 3.1: No. of Students in High QLG Group.

Category

## 3.4.4 Trajectories for DRL policy Induction

Our two DRL policies are induced using pre-collected training data collected by tutor making decisions, and many times, the tutor would produce random yet reasonable decisions. All students used the same probability tutor, followed the same general procedure as described above, studied the same training materials, and worked through the same training problems. It has the same 142 features described above and the same three types of pedagogical decisions but made by the tutor. The two DRL policies differ in the reward function.

The CQL policy induction was offline, containing 2,716 students’ interaction logs over 13 semesters of classroom studies (Fall 2015 to Spring 2022). Its reward function is based on the students’ Normalized Learning Gain (NLG), which measures their learning gain irrespective of their incoming competence. NLG is defined as $\frac{posttest-pretest}{\sqrt{1-pretest}}$ , where 1 is the maximum score for both pre- and post-test.

The CQL-T policy induction was done offline using pre-collected training data containing 1,819 students’ interaction logs over 7 semesters of classroom studies (Spring 2020 to Spring 2023). Its reward function balances NLG and time spent on the tutor.

To evaluate the effectiveness of EM-EDM, we compared it against four AL and two DRL baselines, which include:

 $\bullet$ CQL (Kumar et al. 2020),
While DQN is one of the most widely applied DRL methods, it can suffer from overestimation bias. But Conservative Q-Learning (CQL) (Kumar et al. 2020) is designed to address this overestimation bias in Q-learning. This is why we chose CQL as a DRL baseline.

 $\bullet$ CQL-T (Kumar et al. 2020), which has the same method as CQL except utilizing a reward function that balances performance and time on task. So when two students learn the same, those who spend less time would get higher rewards.

 $\bullet$ BC (Syed and Schapire 2012; Raza et al. 2012; Bain and Sommut 1999), which directly learns a policy using supervised learning on state-action pairs from expert demonstrations. It is a simple approach to learning a policy, but the policy often generalizes poorly and does not recover well from errors.

 $\bullet$ GAIL (Ho and Ermon 2016a), which is a model-free AL algorithm that learns a policy by simultaneously training it with a discriminator that aims to distinguish expert trajectories against trajectories from the learned policy.

 $\bullet$ AIRL (Fu et al. 2018), similar to GAIL, adversarially trains a policy against a discriminator that aims to distinguish the expert demonstrations from the learned policy. Unlike GAIL, AIRL recovers a reward function more generalizable to changes in environment dynamics.

 $\bullet$ EDM (Jarrett et al. 2020a), the state-of-the-art offline AL. It has been demonstrated that EDM can outperform many competitive cutting-edge AL methods with a single reward function, thus we will not repetitively conduct all those comparisons here.

 $\bullet$ EM-EDM (Yang et al. 2023a), our proposed method, which assumes the demonstrations follow multiple reward functions varying across trajectories (while remaining the same within each trajectory).

We compare the effectiveness of the policies induced by our proposed EM-EDM against four AL-based baselines using the 53 demonstrations and two policies induced by DRL using 2,716 and 1,819 student-ITS interactive trajectories, respectively. All the methods were trained and tested on student interaction trajectories through 5-fold cross-validation; as we do not have access to ground truth rewards, we evaluate performance according to action-matching on held-out test data. We used the same held-out test data to evaluate the performance of CQL and CQL-T-induced policies. We employed the metrics of Accuracy (Acc), Recall (Rec), Precision (Prec), F1-score (F1), AUC, APR, and Jaccard score for evaluation. All the model parameters were determined by cross-validation. For EM-EDM, the optimal cluster number was determined heuristically as $4$ for Task 1 and as $5$ for Task 2 by iteratively implementing the EM until empty clusters were generated or the log-likelihood of the clustering results varied smaller than a pre-defined threshold. Based on our observation, the clustering likelihood for EM-EDM converges within $80$ iterations. For fair comparisons, the optimal parameters in other baselines and ablations were also determined by cross-validation.
