Across all evaluation metrics, the proposed HALIDE framework achieves the strongest overall performance, significantly outperforming all flat and hierarchical baselines. In particular, HALIDE trained on expert and sub-optimal data (E+I) attains the highest AUC (0.827) and Jaccard score (0.658), indicating superior discrimination ability and decision-level agreement under substantial cross-semester distribution shift.

Among flat apprenticeship learning baselines trained on expert data (E), EDM consistently outperforms BC, confirming that matching occupancy measures under a learned reward is more effective than direct behavioral cloning. However, flat methods remain limited by their assumption of a single stationary objective and their inability to capture temporal abstraction or evolving pedagogical strategies driven by student agency.

Comparing hierarchical approaches under expert-only data (E), THEMES improves over flat baselines, demonstrating the benefit of time-aware sub-trajectory partitioning and hierarchical policy induction. However, introducing heterogeneous data without quality modeling is insufficient: HALIDE1, trained on mixed data (E+I), does not improve over expert-only THEMES. This suggests that while mixed-quality data expands state-space coverage, unweighted hierarchical learning allows sub-optimal decisions to exert equal influence, diluting informative signals and limiting robustness.

(a) F-score.

(b) Jaccard Score.

Figure 5.4: Critical difference diagram for F-score and Jaccard.

In contrast, incorporating demonstration quality via ranking signal yields consistent gains. Under expert-only data, HALIDE0 outperforms its unweighted counterpart, indicating that even demonstrations labeled as “expert” exhibit meaningful step-level variation in reliability. This challenges the standard apprenticeship learning assumption that expert trajectories are uniformly optimal and highlights the importance of modeling intra-trajectory quality differences in human-centric domains.

The largest gains emerge in the mixed-quality regime. While flat and unweighted hierarchical methods struggle to benefit from heterogeneous demonstrations, HALIDE effectively leverages both expert and sub-optimal data (E+I). By attenuating the influence of low-impact or erroneous decisions while preserving useful routine actions, HALIDE exploits increased state-space coverage without inheriting sub-optimal biases. This selective reuse of heterogeneous data enables HALIDE to substantially outperform both THEMES and weighted flat baselines in all key metrics.

The critical difference (CD) diagrams (Conover and Iman 1979) for F1 and Jaccard scores, shown in Figure 5.4, summarize pairwise statistical comparisons based on the Conover– Friedman test ( $p=0.05$ ) with Holm correction across semester-based folds. Due to the limited number of folds ( $n=4$ ), the test is conservative, and several methods form overlapping equivalence groups despite clear differences in mean performance. Nevertheless, HALIDE consistently attains the best average rank and is clearly separated from weaker baselines such as BC and AHIRL. The absence of separation between some intermediate methods reflects limited statistical power rather than identical performance.

Taken together, these results support the central hypothesis of this work: hierarchical apprenticeship learning benefits from explicitly ranking demonstrations by quality, particularly in realistic educational settings where behavior is heterogeneous, imperfect, and only near-optimal.
HALIDE reconciles robustness with data efficiency by modulating the influence of demonstrated decisions, enabling principled reuse of mixed-quality student data while preserving the temporal and strategic structure induced by hierarchical modeling with evolving rewards.

## 5.6 Results for Healthcare

Table 5.2 reports results across three healthcare datasets—CCHS, Mayo, and MIMIC—under expert-only (E) and all-data (A) training conditions, evaluating both unweighted and quality-weighted variants, with the best-performing method within each block in bold and the overall best results marked with ∗.

Among flat apprenticeship learning baselines trained on expert data (E), THEMES consistently outperforms both EDM and EM-EDM across all three datasets, confirming that temporal abstraction and hierarchical sub-trajectory partitioning are broadly beneficial in clinical decision-making. Notably, EM-EDM yields a more pronounced improvement over EDM on MIMIC than on CCHS or Mayo, suggesting that mixture-based reward modeling offers greater advantage when the dataset is larger and clinician behavior is more heterogeneous.

Incorporating demonstration quality weighting on expert-only data (E) consistently improves hierarchical performance: THEMES w achieves statistically significant gains over unweighted THEMES on CCHS (F1: 0.722 vs. 0.696; AUC: 0.742 vs. 0.725) and on Mayo (F1: 0.679 vs. 0.643; Jaccard: 0.535 vs. 0.490), while remaining competitive on MIMIC. This pattern extends the finding from the educational domain: even demonstrations labeled as expert exhibit meaningful step-level variation in decision reliability, and explicitly modeling this variation improves downstream policy induction.

Expanding to all-quality demonstrations (A) without quality modeling yields mixed results. On MIMIC, THEMES trained on all data achieves AUC = 0.858 and Jaccard = 0.759, surpassing its expert-only counterpart and confirming that broader state-space coverage benefits complex clinical settings. On CCHS, however, augmenting with heterogeneous data under unweighted flat baselines degrades performance in several configurations (e.g., EDMw under A yields F1 = 0.527 vs. 0.622 under E), indicating that undirected inclusion of lower-quality demonstrations can introduce noise that outweighs the benefit of expanded coverage when datasets are smaller.

In contrast, incorporating the quality ranking signal yields consistent gains in the all-data regime. HALIDE trained on all demonstrations achieves the strongest overall results on MIMIC across all four primary metrics: F1 = 0.869, AUC = 0.874, AP = 0.839, and Jaccard = 0.790—each the overall best (∗) in Table 5.2—substantially outperforming both flat and unweighted hierarchical baselines. On Mayo, HALIDE attains the best AUC (0.632∗) and AP (0.559∗), confirming its advantage in discriminative accuracy on moderate-sized datasets. On CCHS, THEMES w trained on expert data remains the top-performing method across most metrics, suggesting that when datasets are smaller and expert demonstrations are relatively sufficient, the marginal gain from leveraging all-quality data diminishes. The dataset-dependent performance pattern—most pronounced on MIMIC and more modest on CCHS—further indicates that the benefit of quality-aware heterogeneous coverage scales with the complexity and heterogeneity of the clinical environment.

Taken together, these results support the central hypothesis of this work across the healthcare domain: hierarchical apprenticeship learning benefits from explicitly ranking demonstrations by quality, particularly on complex, large-scale clinical datasets where clinician behavior is heterogeneous and only near-optimal. HALIDE reconciles the tension between data efficiency and coverage by selectively attenuating the influence of low-quality decisions while preserving useful routine actions, enabling principled reuse of mixed-quality clinical trajectories within a hierarchical framework with evolving rewards.

## 5.7 Discussion, Caveats, and Conclusions

This work addresses a key limitation of AL in human-centric decision-making settings: demonstrations are inherently heterogeneous in quality, even among high-performing demonstrators, whether the decision-maker is a student choosing a pedagogical action or a clinician choosing a treatment. Students differ in prior knowledge, self-regulation, and metacognitive skills, and their decision quality fluctuates over time; clinicians likewise vary in experience and judgment under uncertainty, and their treatment choices vary in effectiveness even within the same case. Both settings violate the standard AL assumption that expert demonstrations are uniformly reliable. Our results show that treating all demonstrations equally injects noise into policy induction, limiting robustness—especially under cross-semester distribution shift in education and across clinical sites in healthcare.

HALIDE operationalizes a principle long established in mixed-initiative ITS research—student agency must be balanced with selective modulation of influence—and shows that the same principle holds for clinician-driven treatment decisions. Rather than discarding imperfect trajectories or uniformly weighting demonstrations, HALIDE integrates continuous demonstration quality signals within a hierarchical apprenticeship learning framework. This allows useful routine decisions from sub-optimal trajectories to contribute while attenuating the influence of low-impact or erroneous actions. Empirically, this integration is critical in both domains: unweighted hierarchical learning fails to benefit from additional heterogeneous data, whereas HALIDE consistently improves performance, with the largest gains observed when leveraging both expert and sub-optimal demonstrations—on ITS data (E+I) and, in the clinical setting, on the largest and most heterogeneous dataset (MIMIC, all-quality data). These results highlight the complementary roles of ranking and hierarchy, where quality-aware weighting stabilizes low-level policy induction and hierarchical structure propagates these improvements to higher-level decision-making strategies, regardless of whether those strategies govern a tutoring session or a course of treatment.

Despite these contributions, this work has several limitations. First, demonstration quality is estimated at the trajectory level and propagated across steps, which provides scalability but only an approximate proxy for step-level effectiveness, in both the pedagogical and clinical settings. Second, while we validate HALIDE across two human-centric domains, all evaluations remain offline, leaving open questions regarding performance under closed-loop deployment; the clinical evaluation is also limited to septic shock prevention, and the educational evaluation to a single ITS, so generalization to other conditions and curricula remains to be tested. Third, the quality ranking mechanism may reflect biases present in historical data—such as differences in student prior preparation and engagement, or in clinician experience and case mix—which could influence policy induction if not carefully monitored.
More broadly, HALIDE relies on the availability of a relative quality ranking to modulate the influence of heterogeneous demonstrations. Although ranking is substantially weaker and easier to obtain than specifying a full reward function, its quality can affect downstream learning. Noisy or misaligned rankings—such as those correlated with short-term performance rather than true pedagogical or clinical effectiveness—may attenuate the benefits of quality-aware weighting. This limitation highlights the importance of careful ranking design and validation when applying HALIDE in practice. Despite these caveats, our findings demonstrate that modeling demonstration quality as a graded structural signal—rather than a binary selection criterion—is essential for robust apprenticeship learning under imperfect human agency, whether that agency belongs to a student or a clinician. By combining quality-aware weighting with hierarchical modeling and evolving rewards, HALIDE offers a principled path toward deploying AL in realistic, heterogeneous human-centric environments spanning education and healthcare.

## Chapter 6 Critical Contrast Hierarchical Apprenticeship Learning

## 6.1 Overview

While apprenticeship learning (AL) has shown promise for inducing effective
decision-making policies directly from human demonstrations in real-world
human-centric environments, most existing approaches assume the demonstrations
are optimal or near-optimal under a single, fixed reward.
Real-world demonstrations, however, are rarely so well-behaved: they are
imperfect, mixing correct and mistaken decisions within the same
trajectory, and inconsistent, since a demonstrator’s underlying
intentions evolve over the course of a task rather than staying fixed.
Compounding this, not all decisions carry equal weight: some occur at routine
states where a wide range of actions succeed, while others occur at critical
junctures where the choice made is pivotal to the outcome.
Existing AL methods address at most one of these gaps at a time, either
treating all timesteps uniformly or weighting entire trajectories only at the
demonstration level, leaving imperfection, evolving intent, and decision-level
criticality unaccounted for jointly.

To close these gaps together, we introduce Cchal (Critical
Contrast Hierarchical Apprenticeship
Learning), a unified framework that builds on existing hierarchical
sub-trajectory clustering and trajectory-level outcome ranking and closes
both remaining gaps jointly: Cchal redistributes each trajectory’s
outcome-quality rank to individual decisions via Bayesian inference in
feature-action space, and separately scores each decision’s criticality by
contrasting expert against non-expert behavior in a dual-arm design—then
combines both signals into a joint per-decision weight that drives weighted
policy induction and sharpens reward learning through a Bradley-Terry
pairwise contrastive IRL objective over observed failure trajectories.

We evaluate Cchal in two challenging human-centric domains: septic shock
prevention across three clinical EHR datasets (CCHS, Mayo Clinic, MIMIC-III)
and student modeling on an intelligent tutoring system (ITS) dataset.
Cchal outperforms every measured baseline on every reported metric on CCHS,
Mayo, and ITS, and is statistically indistinguishable from the strongest prior
baseline on F1 and AUC on MIMIC-III while still exceeding it on APR and
Jaccard—demonstrating that Cchal can accurately induce policies from
imperfect, evolving demonstrations, outperforming competitive baselines and
ablations across a broad range of complex, real-world human-centric tasks.

## 6.2 Introduction

Apprenticeship learning (AL) aims to induce decision-making policies by
observing and imitating human demonstrations, offering a data-driven
alternative to costly or dangerous online trial-and-error in real-world
human-centric domains such as healthcare and education (Abbeel and Ng 2004; Levine et al. 2020).
In healthcare, this problem is especially acute: septic shock kills 270,000
patients per year in the United States alone (Singer et al. 2016), and
clinicians must repeatedly make timely, high-stakes treatment decisions,
making offline apprenticeship learning from electronic health records (EHR) an
attractive route—observe clinician behavior in retrospective records and
recover a policy that imitates the best of it.
But real human demonstrations are far from the ideal expert assumed by
classical apprenticeship learning, and depart from it in two compounding ways.

Demonstrations are imperfect.

A real clinical dataset is a mixture, where some clinicians intervene
optimally at the right moments while others act suboptimally, are late, or are
inconsistent, so even trajectories labeled “positive” (e.g., shock patients
who survived) vary substantially in outcome quality (Wu et al. 2019a; Tangkaratt et al. 2021).

Demonstrations are inconsistent.

A clinician’s intent is not fixed over the course of a case but evolves as the
patient’s condition progresses, so a single trajectory may reflect several
locally distinct decision-making regimes rather than one stationary policy.
Existing offline apprenticeship learning methods for this task, including
THEMES (Yang et al. 2025b), address the second challenge by hierarchically
partitioning each demonstration into sub-trajectory clusters that each capture
a locally consistent regime (Section 6.5.2), and address the first,
at the trajectory level, by quantifying demonstration quality through a
calibrated outcome predictor that produces a scalar rank per trajectory.

Despite this progress, two major limitations remain within this hierarchical
structure.
First, trajectory-level ranks are applied uniformly across all decisions
within each demonstration, even though most decisions occur at stable,
routine states where any reasonable action leads to similar outcomes, while a
minority occur at critical junctures where the treatment choice is pivotal to
whether the patient progresses toward or away from shock—and real clinical
practice is not free of unnecessary intervention at exactly these routine
states: physicians themselves estimate that roughly one-fifth of U.S. medical
care is unnecessary (Lyu et al. 2017), and unnecessarily
broad-spectrum antibiotics given to septic patients who do not need them are
independently associated with a $22\%$ increase in
mortality (Rhee et al. 2020).
To address this, we introduce an outcome-attributed decision weighting that
redistributes the trajectory-level rank to individual decisions via Bayesian
inference in feature-action space (Section 6.5.1).
Second, no existing quality-weighting method identifies which decisions are
behaviorally critical, i.e., where expert and non-expert clinicians choose
differently, as opposed to routine, where any policy would do about as well.
Ju et al. (2021a) identify such states via Q-value spread under a
trained policy, but this requires a converged model and cannot exploit the
contrast between expert and failure demonstrations directly.
To address this, we introduce a dual-arm contrastive criticality score
computed directly from observed behavioral divergence between successful and
failed trajectories, requiring no trained policy (Section 6.5.3).

Decision importance has two complementary dimensions.

The question of which decisions to attend to in apprenticeship learning
therefore has two orthogonal answers, and effective learning from imperfect
demonstrations requires both:

• 

Outcome attribution: How much did decision $t$ contribute to
trajectory $i$ ’s observed outcome quality?
A decision at a stable state, even in an expert trajectory, contributes
little; a decision at a physiological inflection point may determine the
outcome.

• 

Decision criticality: How sharply does decision $t$ separate
expert from non-expert clinical behavior at this state?
A decision that both groups make identically—however it affects the
outcome—provides no contrastive learning signal.

These are not the same signal: a decision can be outcome-attributable but not
critical (an expert maneuver at a state where non-experts happen to act
similarly), or critical but from a low-quality trajectory (a pivotal choice
made incorrectly).
No prior framework answers both questions jointly: trajectory-level
quality-weighting methods (Wu et al. 2019a; Tangkaratt et al. 2021; Yang et al. 2025b)
answer only the first, and Q-value-based critical-state
methods (Ju et al. 2021a; Roth et al. 2019; Eysenbach et al. 2019)
answer only the second, and only when a trained policy is already available.

We propose Critical Contrast Hierarchical Apprenticeship Learning (Cchal),
a unified framework that, building on THEMES’s hierarchical sub-trajectory
clustering, answers both questions and combines them into a single joint
per-decision attention weight (Section 6.5.4), which in turn
scales a Bradley-Terry pairwise contrastive IRL objective that sharpens reward
learning at the decision points where expert-versus-failure contrast is
strongest (Section 6.5.5).
To the best of our knowledge, Cchal is the first apprenticeship learning
framework to jointly attribute demonstration quality to individual decisions
and score their behavioral criticality without a trained policy or an
environment reward signal—only the observable contrast between successful
and failed demonstrations, and a trajectory-level demonstration rank derived
from a calibrated outcome predictor.
Setting criticality to uniform and GP credit to the trajectory-level rank
recovers a trajectory-level-only special case, making our ablations directly
interpretable (Section 6.6.4).
We evaluate Cchal across two human-centric domains, following THEMES’s
original healthcare-and-education scope (Yang et al. 2025b): septic shock
prevention on three clinical EHR datasets and student modeling on one
intelligent tutoring system (ITS) dataset.
The same two-dimensional decision importance holds in both: just as clinical
decisions range from routine monitoring to life-or-death inflection points,
tutoring decisions range from routine problem steps to moments that determine
whether a struggling student disengages or a high performer plateaus.

Our contributions are:

• 

We introduce outcome-attributed decision weighting
(Section 6.5.1), repurposing the Bayesian Reward Distribution
(BRD) framework (Azizsoltani and Jin 2019) in feature-action space so
that clinically similar state-action pairs receive proportional attention
regardless of temporal position.

• 

We introduce dual-arm contrastive criticality scoring
(Section 6.5.3), which estimates local action distributions
via $k$ -nearest neighbours in a stratum-matched design—contrasting
 $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ vs. $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ and $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ vs. $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ —so that criticality is measured against the same
initial risk stratum, requiring no trained policy, cluster label, or MDP
model.

• 

We combine both into a joint decision-attention weight
(Section 6.5.4) that scales a Bradley-Terry pairwise
contrastive IRL objective (Section 6.5.5), using
observed failure trajectories as negatives to sharpen reward learning at
the decision points where expert-versus-failure contrast is strongest.

• 

We evaluate Cchal against seven baselines, including THEMES,
its direct methodological predecessor, on three clinical EHR datasets for
septic shock prevention and one intelligent tutoring system (ITS) dataset
for student modeling, showing that the outcome-attribution-plus-criticality
principle is not specific to septic shock.

## 6.3 Related Work

Apprenticeship Learning and Imitation from Imperfect Demonstrations.

Classical apprenticeship learning (Abbeel and Ng 2004) recovers a
reward function that makes expert demonstrations near-optimal, assuming
demonstrations come from a single, uniformly expert source.
Maximum Entropy IRL (Ziebart et al. 2008) and its deep extensions
(AIRL (Fu et al. 2018), GAIL (Ho and Ermon 2016b)) share this
assumption.
SQIL (Reddy et al. 2020) and IQL (Kostrikov et al. 2022) operate
offline but treat all demonstration timesteps uniformly.
T-REX (Brown et al. 2019) relaxes the expert assumption by
learning a reward consistent with a ranking over demonstrations, and
DemoDICE (Kim et al. 2022b) handles imperfect demonstrations offline via
divergence minimization.
Both work at the trajectory level and do not assign differential importance
to individual decisions within a demonstration.

Demonstration Quality in IRL.

Several works weight demonstrations by quality: Wu et al. (2019a) use a
confidence-weighted BC objective; Sasaki and Yamashina (2021a) filter
low-quality demos; Tangkaratt et al. (2021) model the demonstrator’s
expertise level.
Closest to ours, THEMES (Yang et al. 2025b) uses trajectory-level quality
from an outcome predictor but applies it uniformly across all timesteps of
each demonstration, conflating within-trajectory temporal heterogeneity.
Halide (Islam et al. 2026) introduces a calibrated
demonstration-rank framework in the same spirit, validated on intelligent
tutoring systems; Cchal adapts this rank mechanism to the clinical domain
(Section 6.5.1) and, unlike Halide, decomposes it within a
trajectory and contrasts expert against non-expert behavior rather than
applying it uniformly.

Outcome Attribution in Demonstrations.

Temporal credit assignment in RL asks how to distribute environment reward to
timesteps within a trajectory (Sutton and Barto 2018).
In apprenticeship learning the analogous question is distinct: given a
trajectory’s observed outcome quality (not an environment reward),
which individual decisions are most responsible for it?
InferGP (Azizsoltani and Jin 2019) uses GP inference to distribute
delayed environment rewards, and InferNet (Ausin et al. 2021) extends
this via neural estimation for ShortTR computation in Critical-DRL.
Neither work applies outcome attribution to demonstration quality in
an imitation learning setting.
Cchal repurposes the GP inference mechanism for this purpose: treating
trajectory quality as the observation and feature-action similarity as the
kernel structure naturally produces per-decision weights that reflect each
decision’s contribution to the observed outcome—without requiring any
environment reward signal.

Critical State Identification.

Ju et al. (2021a) define critical states via Q-value spread (LongTR)
and inferred immediate rewards (ShortTR), requiring a trained DQN.
Decision Points (Roth et al. 2019) use value function uncertainty.
Bottleneck states (Eysenbach et al. 2019) exploit graph connectivity.
Cchal defines criticality without any trained policy, using only the
observed behavioral contrast between success and failure trajectories.

Overtreatment as a Motivation for Criticality-Aware Learning.

A separate clinical literature documents that a substantial share of
intervention is medically unnecessary: physicians estimate roughly one-fifth
of U.S. care is overtreatment, most often driven by diagnostic uncertainty
and defensive practice rather than patient need (Lyu et al. 2017).
In sepsis specifically, unnecessarily broad-spectrum empiric
antibiotics—prescribed to patients who do not harbor resistant
organisms—are independently associated with higher
mortality (Rhee et al. 2020), and in the ICU, structured protocols
that help clinicians identify which patients genuinely warrant continued
escalation reduce invasive treatment and length of stay without harming
outcomes (Chang et al. 2021), indicating that a meaningful share of
aggressive intervention is separable from patient benefit.
This literature has not, to our knowledge, previously been connected to
critical-state identification in apprenticeship learning: prior
work (Ju et al. 2021a; Roth et al. 2019; Eysenbach et al. 2019)
motivates criticality only as a way to avoid missing pivotal decisions, i.e.,
only against undertreatment.
Cchal’s criticality score is low both when experts and non-experts act
identically at a stable state and, by construction, at states where
behavioral contrast is weak for any reason—including states where
aggressive intervention is common in the demonstrations but not clearly
linked to outcome.
Because the joint weight (Eq. (6.10)) concentrates learning
on high-criticality decisions and correspondingly de-emphasizes
low-criticality ones, Cchal is less prone than uniformly-weighted
baselines to reproducing whatever unnecessary intervention appears in the
demonstrations at routine states, offering a plausible mechanism against
overtreatment alongside its primary role of concentrating signal where it is
outcome-relevant; verifying this directly would require action-level
overtreatment labels beyond the shock/non-shock outcome data used here.

Contrastive and Adversarial IRL.

AIRL (Fu et al. 2018) and f-IRL (Ni et al. 2021) use adversarial
training between expert and generated trajectories, but in an online
setting.
Kim et al. (2022b) use offline contrastive divergence minimization.
Cchal differs by using observed failure demonstrations (not
generated ones) as negatives, and scaling the contrastive signal by
per-state criticality.

Clinical RL.

Raghu et al. (2017a) apply DQN to sepsis treatment; Komorowski et al. (2018)
use a continuous-state approach.
THEMES (Yang et al. 2025b) introduces hierarchical offline apprenticeship
learning for EHR with trajectory-level quality weighting; Cchal extends
this line by adding outcome-attributed per-decision weighting, contrastive
criticality scoring, and Bradley-Terry pairwise IRL—moving from
trajectory-level attention to decision-level attention.

## 6.4 Preliminaries

Problem Setting.

Let $\mathcal{S}$ be a state space and $\mathcal{A}$ a discrete action space.
We observe a dataset $\mathcal{D}=\{\tau_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}$ of trajectories
collected from clinical practice, where
 $\tau_{i}=(s_{0}^{i},a_{0}^{i},s_{1}^{i},a_{1}^{i},\ldots,s_{n_{i}}^{i})$ .
Each trajectory $\tau_{i}$ has an associated delayed outcome
 $y_{i}\in\{0,1\}$ (1 = septic shock, 0 = no shock) and a demonstration rank
 $q_{i}\in(0,1)$ , higher values indicating higher-quality behavior leading to
non-shock outcomes.

Trajectories are partitioned along two independent binary axes. The first, a
risk stratum (hi/lo), is the patient’s state before treatment—at
risk of shock, or not. The second is the eventual outcome $y_{i}$ —did the
patient overcome that risk (or simply remain stable, if not at risk), or did
they stay at risk and progress to shock. Crossing the two yields four groups
(Table 6.1): a target population, whose outcome was
favorable, which Cchal tries to emulate; and a reference
population, whose outcome was shock, used only as a contrast baseline.

Table 6.1: Notation for the four demonstration groups.

Target ( $+$ )
Reference ( $-$ )

Hi-risk (at risk pre-treatment)
 $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ overcame risk
 $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ stayed at risk, shock

Lo-risk (not at risk pre-treatment)
 $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ remained stable
 $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ deteriorated, shock

MT-TICC State Clustering.

Multi-task TICC (Hallac et al. 2017) segments each trajectory into a
sequence of discrete state labels $k_{t}^{i}\in\{0,\ldots,K-1\}$ , where $K$ is
the number of temporal state clusters (default $K=9$ ). This provides a shared
state representation across patients.

EM-EDM Policy Learning.

The EM-EDM module (Yang et al. 2025b) jointly assigns trajectory sub-segments
to behavior clusters and trains a per-cluster behavior model (EDM student),
iterating between cluster assignment (E-step) and model update (M-step). The
log-likelihood objective is:

 $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{EDM}}=\sum_{\tau_{i}}\sum_{t}\log P(a_{t}^{i}\mid s_{t}^{i};\,\theta_{z_{t}^{i}})$ 

(6.1)

where $z_{t}^{i}$ is the cluster assignment for timestep $t$ of trajectory $i$ .

MLIRL Reward Learning.

The IRL phase uses maximum-likelihood IRL (MLIRL) (Babes et al. 2011)
to recover a linear reward function $R(s,a)=\theta^{\top}\phi(s,a)$ that
maximizes the Boltzmann policy likelihood of the expert demonstrations under
the induced MDP.

## 6.5 The Cchal Framework

Figure 6.1: 
Cchal overview. Trajectory-level demonstration ranks are
distributed to per-timestep weights via InferGP Bayesian credit
assignment in feature-action space (top left; pre-computed, stored as
w_t_gp).
Dual-arm contrastive criticality scores are estimated from kNN local JSD
in clinical feature space (top right; built once per repeat): the
high-risk arm contrasts $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ vs. $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ and the
low-risk arm contrasts $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ vs. $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ , each
anchored to the same initial risk stratum. Phase 3 weighted EM-EDM uses
 $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}\cup\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ only (failures excluded from the
generative model).
Phase 4 BT-IRL uses $\mathcal{D}^{+}=\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}\cup\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ 
and $\mathcal{D}^{-}=\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{hi}}\cup\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ with
arm-routed action-aware weights (bottom).

Figure 6.1 presents an overview of Cchal, with further
details provided in Algorithm 3.
Cchal proceeds in five phases: Phase 0 distributes trajectory-level
demonstration ranks to per-decision outcome-attribution weights via InferGP
Bayesian credit assignment in feature-action space
(Section 6.5.1); Phase 1 partitions each demonstration
into sub-trajectory clusters via MT-TICC (Section 6.5.2); Phase
2 scores each decision’s criticality via dual-arm contrastive kNN-JSD
(Section 6.5.3); Phase 3 combines both signals into a
joint per-decision weight that drives weighted EM-EDM policy induction
(Section 6.5.4); and Phase 4 uses the same joint
weight to scale a Bradley-Terry pairwise contrastive IRL objective for reward
learning (Section 6.5.5).

## 6.5.1 Phase 0: Outcome-Attributed Decision Weighting

Given that trajectory $i$ achieved outcome quality $q_{i}$ , we ask: which
individual decisions within the trajectory contributed most to that quality?
This is outcome attribution—a question about decision importance in
apprenticeship learning, distinct from RL temporal credit assignment which
requires an environment reward signal.
We answer it by repurposing the Bayesian Reward Distribution (BRD) framework
of Azizsoltani and Jin (2019): treating $q_{i}$ as the observation and an
RBF kernel over feature-action vectors $\phi(s,a)$ as the prior, GP inference
distributes $q_{i}$ across the trajectory’s decisions in proportion to their
feature-action similarity to the trajectory’s high-quality moments.
Crucially, the kernel operates in feature-action space: two decisions
at clinically similar state-action configurations receive similar
attribution regardless of when they occur in a trajectory.
The resulting per-decision weights are computed once offline and stored as
the column w_t_gp in the EHR data file.

Feature-action representation.

For each event $(s_{t}^{i},a_{t}^{i})$ we form a feature-action vector
 $\phi(s_{t}^{i},a_{t}^{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{2F}$ by concatenating the $F$ -dimensional
clinical state features with a one-hot action embedding.

RBF kernel and mean-constraint observation model.

We place a GP prior over the per-timestep quality function $r:\phi\mapsto\mathbb{R}$ 
with an RBF kernel:

 $K(\phi,\phi^{\prime})=\exp\!\left(-\frac{\|\phi-\phi^{\prime}\|^{2}}{2\ell^{2}}\right).$ 

(6.2)

The trajectory-level rank is linked to per-timestep weights via a
length-invariant mean constraint:

 $q_{i}=\frac{1}{n_{i}}\sum_{t\in\tau_{i}}r_{t}+\varepsilon_{i},\qquad\varepsilon_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\,\alpha).$ 

(6.3)

Equivalently, $q_{i}=\mathbf{D}_{i}\mathbf{r}+\varepsilon_{i}$ where
 $D_{i}[t]=1/n_{i}$ if $t\in\tau_{i}$ and $0$ otherwise. Using a mean rather than
a discounted sum makes the observation model length-invariant: short and long
trajectories contribute on the same scale.

Joint posterior inference.

Writing $\ell$ for the total number of events across all trajectories and $m$ 
for the number of trajectories, the BRD posterior mean over all events is:

 $\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{GP}}_{\mathrm{raw}}=\mathbf{K}\mathbf{D}^{\top}\underbrace{\left(\mathbf{D}\mathbf{K}\mathbf{D}^{\top}+\alpha\mathbf{I}_{m}\right)^{-1}}_{\text{$m{\times}m$, Cholesky}}\mathbf{q}$ 

(6.4)

where $\mathbf{K}\mathbf{D}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell\times m}$ is built in
trajectory-sized chunks (column $j$ = $n_{j}^{-1}\sum_{t\in\tau_{j}}K(\cdot,\phi_{t}^{j})$ )
without materialising the full $\ell\times\ell$ kernel, keeping peak memory at
 $O(\ell m)$ . The $m\times m$ system is solved by Cholesky factorisation.

Normalisation.

Raw GP values are z-scored and passed through a sigmoid:

 $w^{\mathrm{GP}}_{t}=\sigma\!\left(\frac{w^{\mathrm{GP}}_{\mathrm{raw},t}-\mu_{\mathrm{raw}}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{raw}}}\right)\in(0,1)$ 

(6.5)

ensuring weights are bounded and comparable across trajectories and datasets.
Inference is joint over all training trajectories ( $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ ,
 $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ , $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ , and $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ when
available), so $r(s,a_{\mathrm{pos}})$ is naturally higher than
 $r(s,a_{\mathrm{neg}})$ at contrastive states—a built-in property of the
feature-action kernel that would be absent from a temporal kernel.

## 6.5.2 Phase 1: MT-TICC Temporal State Clustering

MT-TICC is run on training trajectories only (inductive mode) to assign each
timestep to a discrete state cluster $k_{t}\in\{0,\ldots,K-1\}$ . This
clustering step is unchanged from standard inductive MT-TICC practice and is
not further modified by Cchal.

## 6.5.3 Phase 2: Dual-Arm Contrastive Critical State Scoring

Rather than computing a single criticality score per MT-TICC cluster,
Cchal estimates continuous per-timestep criticality scores directly
in clinical feature space via $k$ -nearest neighbours. Crucially, the
contrasting populations are stratum-matched: we build two independent
arms whose positive and negative pools share the same initial clinical risk
profile (pre-shock vs. non-shock), preventing cross-stratum JSD from
conflating physiologically distinct populations.

High-risk arm ( $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ vs. $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ ).

Among patients who were at risk of septic shock, we contrast those who
recovered ( $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ , the expert pool) against those who did not
( $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ , the failure pool).
For each training timestep $t$ with feature vector $s_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{F}$ :

 $\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}(s_{t})=k\text{-NN of }s_{t}\text{ in }\bigl\{s_{t^{\prime}}^{i}:i\in\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}\bigr\},\quad\mathcal{N}^{-}_{\mathrm{hi}}(s_{t})=k\text{-NN of }s_{t}\text{ in }\bigl\{s_{t^{\prime}}^{i}:i\in\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{hi}}\bigr\}.$ 

(6.6)

Local action distributions $P^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}(a\mid s_{t})$ and $P^{-}_{\mathrm{hi}}(a\mid s_{t})$ 
are estimated with Laplace smoothing:

 $P^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}(a\mid s_{t})\;\propto\;\#\{t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}(s_{t}):a_{t^{\prime}}=a\}+1,$ 

(6.7)

and analogously for $P^{-}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ .
The high-risk criticality score is:

 $\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{hi}}(s_{t})=\frac{\mathrm{JSD}\!\bigl(P^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}(\cdot\mid s_{t})\;\|\;P^{-}_{\mathrm{hi}}(\cdot\mid s_{t})\bigr)}{\log 2}\in[0,1].$ 

(6.8)

Low-risk arm ( $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ vs. $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ ).

Among patients who were not initially at shock risk, we contrast those
who remained stable ( $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ ) against those who deteriorated into
shock ( $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ ). These two populations share the same non-shock
starting condition, so their behavioral divergence measures de novo
risk escalation.
When both $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ and $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ are present in the
training fold:

 $\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}}(s_{t})=k\text{-NN of }s_{t}\text{ in }\bigl\{s_{t^{\prime}}^{i}:i\in\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}}\bigr\},\quad\mathcal{N}^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}}(s_{t})=k\text{-NN of }s_{t}\text{ in }\bigl\{s_{t^{\prime}}^{i}:i\in\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}}\bigr\}.$ 

(6.9)

The low-risk criticality score $\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{lo}}(s_{t})$ is defined analogously to
Eq. (6.8).

Arm routing.

Each trajectory receives scores from the arm that matches its initial
stratum: $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ trajectories use $\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{hi}}(\cdot)$ ; $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ 
trajectories use $\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{lo}}(\cdot)$ .
This ensures the criticality signal captures within-stratum behavioral
divergence, not a mixture of physiologically distinct starting conditions.
When the low-risk arm is inactive (only $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ / $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ 
data available), Cchal falls back to a single $\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{hi}}(\cdot)$ arm.

Sparse support and normalisation.

For timesteps with fewer than $m_{\min}$ neighbours in either pool, we
substitute the dataset-mean score for that arm ( $\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ 
or $\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ ). Within each arm, scores are
independently rescaled so that the per-arm maximum equals 1.

Both sets of kNN indices are built once per repeat before the outer EM
loop, independent of MT-TICC cluster labels. Criticality therefore does not
change across EM iterations, eliminating the instability that cluster-level
schemes exhibit when TICC labels shift.

Action-aware local probabilities.

Each arm also yields per-timestep action probability estimates used in
Phase 4: $P^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}(a_{t}\mid s_{t})$ , $P^{-}_{\mathrm{hi}}(a_{t}\mid s_{t})$ ,
 $P^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}}(a_{t}\mid s_{t})$ , and $P^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}}(a_{t}\mid s_{t})$ —the
local probability that a demonstrator from the respective group would choose
the action $a_{t}$ actually taken. These serve as arm-routed, action-aware
scaling factors in the BT-IRL objective.

## 6.5.4 Phase 3: Joint Critical-Quality Weighting and EM-EDM

Data selection for EM-EDM.

EM-EDM is restricted to the positive quadrants
 $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}\cup\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ .
Trajectories from $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ and $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ are deliberately
excluded: including failure-mode demonstrations in a generative behavior
model risks contaminating behavior clusters with anti-expert action patterns
that the downstream IRL objective cannot fully disentangle at the
cluster-granularity level.
 $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ and $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ enter only in Phase 4 as contrast
negatives.

Arm-routed joint weight.

For each training timestep $t$ in the EM-EDM training set, the joint weight
is:

 $w_{t}=w^{\mathrm{GP}}_{t}\times\begin{cases}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{hi}}(s_{t})&\text{if }\tau_{i}\in\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}},\\
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{lo}}(s_{t})&\text{if }\tau_{i}\in\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}},\end{cases}$ 

(6.10)

where $w^{\mathrm{GP}}_{t}\in(0,1)$ is the InferGP credit weight loaded from
w_t_gp.

This weight combines how good is this trajectory at this moment
(InferGP credit) with how much does the action choice here distinguish
success from failure within the same initial risk stratum (arm-routed kNN
JSD criticality).
Only high-quality demonstrations at genuinely critical feature-space
states—measured against a physiologically appropriate contrast group—have
maximum influence on policy learning.

The weighted EM-EDM log-likelihood is:

 $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{CCHAL\text{-}EDM}}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}\cup\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}}}\sum_{t}w_{t}\cdot\log P(a_{t}^{i}\mid s_{t}^{i};\,\theta_{z_{t}^{i}})$ 

(6.11)

The E-step cluster assignment probabilities $\rho_{iz}$ and M-step student
model updates both use $w_{t}$ as importance weights throughout.

## 6.5.5 Phase 4: Bradley-Terry Pairwise Contrastive IRL

We learn a linear reward $\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}||\mathcal{A}|}$ 
over the high-level (MT-TICC state, EM cluster) feature space by maximising a
Bradley-Terry pairwise preference objective (Christiano et al. 2017; Brown et al. 2019)
that directly enforces positive returns to exceed negative returns across all
trajectory pairs.

Positive and negative sets.

The full set of available demonstrations is partitioned as:
 $\mathcal{D}^{+}=\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}\cup\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ (experts and stable
non-shock patients) and $\mathcal{D}^{-}=\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{hi}}\cup\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ 
(shock failures and non-shock-to-shock deteriorations). Using all four
quadrants in BT-IRL—while excluding failures from EM-EDM—maximises the
contrastive signal without corrupting the generative behavior model.

 $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{BT}}(\theta)=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{D}^{+}}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{D}^{-}}w^{+}_{i}\cdot w^{-}_{j}\cdot\log\sigma\!\left(J(\tau^{+}_{i};\,\theta)-J(\tau^{-}_{j};\,\theta)\right)$ 

(6.12)

where $\sigma$ is the sigmoid, $w^{\pm}$ are normalised per-trajectory weights,
and the action-aware weighted return is:

 $J(\tau;\,\theta)\;=\;\theta^{\top}\widetilde{\Phi}(\tau),\qquad\widetilde{\Phi}(\tau)\;=\;\sum_{t\in\tau}w(s_{t},a_{t})\,\phi(s_{t},a_{t})$ 

(6.13)

Arm-routed action-aware weights.

The per-step weights route each trajectory to the arm whose contrast pool
matches its initial stratum:

 $\displaystyle w^{+}(s_{t},a_{t})$ 
 $\displaystyle=w^{\mathrm{GP}}_{t}\times\begin{cases}P^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}(a_{t}\mid s_{t})&\text{if }\tau_{i}\in\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}},\\
P^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}}(a_{t}\mid s_{t})&\text{if }\tau_{i}\in\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}},\end{cases}$ 

(6.14)

 $\displaystyle w^{-}(s_{t},a_{t})$ 
 $\displaystyle=w^{\mathrm{GP}}_{t}\times\begin{cases}P^{-}_{\mathrm{hi}}(a_{t}\mid s_{t})&\text{if }\tau_{j}\in\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{hi}},\\
P^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}}(a_{t}\mid s_{t})&\text{if }\tau_{j}\in\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}}.\end{cases}$ 

(6.15)

Under the Bradley-Terry model, $\sigma(J^{+}_{i}-J^{-}_{j})$ is the probability that
expert trajectory $i$ is preferred over negative trajectory $j$ . Maximising
Eq. (6.12) jointly pushes $J(\tau^{+})>J(\tau^{-})$ across
all pairs simultaneously.

Why BT rather than the additive form.

The alternative $\mathcal{L}^{+}-\lambda\mathcal{L}^{-}$ (maximise expert
Boltzmann likelihood and independently minimise negative likelihood)
conflates two separate objectives that can trade off unpredictably and
requires tuning $\lambda$ to balance them.
The BT objective has a single consistent interpretation—preference
probability over pairs—and is parameter-free once weights are set.

The gradient of Eq. (6.12) is:

 $\nabla_{\theta}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{BT}}=\sum_{i,j}w^{+}_{i}w^{-}_{j}\cdot\underbrace{\sigma(J^{-}_{j}-J^{+}_{i})}_{\text{misprediction prob.}}\cdot\bigl(\widetilde{\Phi}^{+}_{i}-\widetilde{\Phi}^{-}_{j}\bigr)$ 

(6.16)

Pairs where the negative currently beats the positive ( $J^{-}_{j}>J^{+}_{i}$ ,
misprediction probability $\approx 1$ ) contribute the largest gradient,
pulling $\theta$ to increase $J^{+}$ and decrease $J^{-}$ .
We minimise $-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{BT}}$ using L-BFGS-B with bounds
 $\theta\in[-1,1]^{|\mathcal{S}||\mathcal{A}|}$ .

When no negative demos are available (e.g., $\lambda_{\mathrm{contrast}}=0$ ),
Cchal falls back to standard MLIRL on positive demos; combined with
uniform criticality this recovers a trajectory-level-only baseline exactly.

## 6.5.6 Full Algorithm

Algorithm 3 summarizes Cchal end-to-end.

Algorithm 3 Cchal: Critical Contrast Hierarchical Apprenticeship Learning.

 0:

  Dataset $\mathcal{D}$ , demonstration ranks $\{q_{i}\}$ ,
pre-computed InferGP weights $w^{\mathrm{GP}}_{t}$ (col w_t_gp,
via Eqs. (6.4)–(6.5)),
quadrant indices $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}},\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{hi}},\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}},\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ ,
min support $m_{\min}$ , contrast weight $\lambda$ 

 1:

  // Phase 0: InferGP per-timestep weights (pre-computed, loaded)

 2:

  Load $w^{\mathrm{GP}}_{t}$ from w_t_gp for all events $t$ 

 3:

  for each repeat $r=1,\ldots,R$ do

 4:

   Sample train/test split; extract $\{{\tau}_{i}^{\mathrm{tr}}\}$ 

 5:

   // Phase 1: MT-TICC (inductive, on train only)

 6:

   Run MT-TICC on $\{{\tau}_{i}^{\mathrm{tr}}\}$ ; obtain labels $\{k_{t}^{i}\}$ 

 7:

   // Phase 2: Dual-arm kNN Criticality (built once per repeat)

 8:

   High-risk arm: Build kNN indices over $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ (positive) and $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ (negative) training events

 9:

   for each training timestep $t$ do

 10:

    Compute $\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{hi}}(s_{t})$ via Eqs. (6.6)–(6.8)
 (fallback $\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ if support $<m_{\min}$ )

 11:

   end for

 12:

   if $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ and $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ present in train fold then

 13:

    Low-risk arm: Build kNN indices over $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ and $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ training events

 14:

    for each $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ training timestep $t$ do

 15:

     Compute $\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{lo}}(s_{t})$ via Eq. (6.9) analogously

 16:

    end for

 17:

   end if

 18:

   // Phase 3: Weighted EM-EDM (Q10 + Q00 only)

 19:

   Restrict training demos to $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}\cup\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ 

 20:

   Compute arm-routed $w_{t}$ via Eq. (6.10) for all $t$ 

 21:

   Partition sub-trajectories; initialize cluster assignments

 22:

   for each EM iteration do

 23:

    E-step: Assign sub-trajectories to behavior clusters using $w_{t}$ 

 24:

    M-step: Update EDM student models via Eq. (6.11)

 25:

    Evaluate on test split; record metrics

 26:

   end for

 27:

   // Phase 4: Contrastive IRL ( $\mathcal{D}^{+}=\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}\cup\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ ,
 $\mathcal{D}^{-}=\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{hi}}\cup\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ )

 28:

   Compute discrete demos using cluster assignments from Phase 3

 29:

   Compute $\widetilde{\Phi}^{\pm}(\tau)$ via arm-routed weights Eqs. (6.14)–(6.13)

 30:

   Solve $\min_{\theta}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{BT}}(\theta)$ via L-BFGS-B (Eq. (6.12))

 31:

   Update reward weights; re-run MT-TICC with reward regularization

 32:

  end for

 33:

  return Best policy by AUC over repeats and EM iterations

## 6.6 Experiments

## 6.6.1 Datasets and Evaluation

We evaluate Cchal on three clinical EHR datasets for septic shock
prevention, and—following THEMES (Yang et al. 2025b), whose original
scope spanned both healthcare and education—one intelligent tutoring
system (ITS) dataset for student modeling, to test whether the
outcome-attribution-plus-criticality principle generalizes beyond the
clinical domain:

CCHS (Christiana Care Health System): 1,863 shock trajectories and
1,863 non-shock trajectories; 14 sepsis-related features (vital signs and lab
results); mean trajectory length 34, max 507.

Mayo Clinic: 2,555 positive and 2,555 negative trajectories; 14
clinical measurements; mean length 65, max 1,160.

MIMIC-III (Johnson et al. 2016): Widely used public ICU
benchmark; preprocessed following Raghu et al. (2017a); 14 features; 8,188
trajectories (4,094 shock, 4,094 non-shock).

ITS (Pyrenees Intelligent Tutoring System): An undergraduate
probability ITS deployed to over 3,000 students, generating approximately 1
million interaction logs over 11 academic years.
Since students’ underlying learning state is unobservable (Mandel and others 2014),
states are represented by 130-dimensional continuous features covering
autonomy, timing, problem-solving context, performance, and hint usage;
actions are the three-way choice of solving a problem independently, studying
a worked solution, or collaborating with the tutor.
Expert demonstrations are the 89 student trajectories with high Quantized
Learning Gain (QLG) (Mao et al. 2018), a binary indicator of
pre-test-to-post-test improvement.
This is the same dataset on which Halide (Islam et al. 2026)
validated its demonstration-rank framework; Cchal evaluates whether adding
dual-arm criticality and BT-IRL improves further on that result, in addition
to testing the framework’s first clinical application.
For Cchal, the same demonstration-rank and $\mathcal{Q}$ -quadrant pipeline
used on the clinical datasets is applied with QLG in place of the
shock/non-shock outcome label.

All clinical datasets use a demonstration rank derived from an offline
shock-outcome predictor; ITS uses QLG as described above.
We report Accuracy, AUC-ROC, AUPRC, F-score, and Jaccard averaged over 10
random train/test splits (80/20). Statistical significance is assessed by
paired Wilcoxon signed-rank tests ( $p<0.05$ ).

The three clinical datasets and the ITS dataset are shared with
THEMES (Yang et al. 2025b); we summarize their collection and
preprocessing here for completeness, since Cchal’s quadrant partition
(Section 6.5.3) and demonstration rank
(Section 6.5.1) are both built directly on top of them.

Healthcare Datasets

Data Collection.

MIMIC-III (Medical Information Mart for Intensive Care—Version Three).

A large, freely accessible database of patients admitted to critical care
units from 2001 to 2012 (Singer et al. 2016), widely used in prior
offline apprenticeship-learning work on sepsis
treatment (Jarrett et al. 2020a; Yang et al. 2025b).
Like CCHS and Mayo, it includes vital signs, medications, laboratory
measurements, diagnostic codes, hospital length of stay, and survival data,
but—unlike CCHS and Mayo—it is restricted exclusively to ICU visits
(Section 6.7).
We include all visits from 2001 to 2012, totaling 53,423 visits.

CCHS.

A non-profit healthcare system, from which we collected EHRs from July 2013
to December 2015: 119,968 hospitalized patients (age $\geq 18$ ) across
210,289 emergency-department visits, with length of stay ranging from 30
minutes to 3 years.

Mayo Clinic.

A network of non-profit hospitals, comprising 232,000 patients and 336,500
visits from 2011 to 2016.
To match the CCHS collection window we restrict to July 2013–December 2015
(83,034 patients, 121,019 visits; length of stay 1 day to 1.5 years),
excluding surgical visits and patients under 18 to remain consistent with
CCHS.

Data Preprocessing.

Feature selection.

Following two leading clinicians from CCHS and Mayo, we use 14 features
related to sepsis progression: vital signs—systolic blood
pressure, mean arterial pressure, respiratory rate, oxygen saturation, heart
rate, temperature, and fraction of inspired oxygen; and lab
results—white blood cell count, bilirubin, blood urea nitrogen, lactate,
creatinine, platelet count, and neutrophil bands.

Missing data.

Features are recorded at irregular intervals ranging from seconds to days,
so most timestamps have missing entries: the average missing rate is
 $\sim$ 80% for CCHS and Mayo and $\sim$ 50% for MIMIC-III.
We forward-fill and impute any remaining gaps with the per-feature mean, a
standard and robust approach for EHR missingness in septic-shock
early-prediction tasks (Kim and Chi 2018; Zhang et al. 2019).

Tagging septic shock.

Following the Third International Consensus Definitions for Sepsis and
Septic Shock (Sepsis-3) (Singer et al. 2016), two leading clinicians
from CCHS and Mayo tagged a visit as septic shock when either (i) persistent
hypotension held across two consecutive readings $\leq 30$ minutes
apart—systolic blood pressure $<90$ mmHg, mean arterial pressure $<65$ 
mmHg, or a systolic drop $\geq 40$ mmHg within 8 hours—or (ii) any
vasopressor was administered.
Intersecting this rule with ICD-9 codes identifies 4,094, 1,863, and 2,555
septic-shock visits in MIMIC-III, CCHS, and Mayo respectively.

Quadrant partitioning.

The risk-stratum/outcome partition used throughout this chapter
(Section 6.4, Table 6.1) crosses the
patient’s pre-treatment risk assessment against their actual outcome:
patients at risk of shock before treatment who go on to overcome that risk
join the hi-risk target population ( $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ ); those
who stay at risk and progress to shock join the hi-risk reference population
( $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ ); patients not initially at risk who remain
stable join the lo-risk target population ( $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ );
and those who nonetheless deteriorate into shock join the lo-risk reference
population ( $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ ).
The resulting quadrant sizes for each dataset are reported above.

Education (ITS) Dataset

Data Collection.

The Pyrenees Intelligent Tutoring System has been deployed to over 3,000
undergraduates over 11 academic years, generating approximately 1 million
interaction logs.
Our experimental cohort draws from 221 of these students across four
semesters: Spring 2021 (67 students), Spring 2022 (56), Spring 2024 (54),
and Fall 2024 (44).
Each student proceeds through four stages: studying foundational
probability concepts from a textbook (explanations, worked examples, and
single- and multi-principle practice problems); an 8-problem pre-test; a
training phase with 12 problems presented in a fixed sequence, at each of
which the student chooses to solve independently, study a worked solution,
or collaborate with the tutor; and a 12-problem post-test.
The 89 high-QLG trajectories used as Cchal’s expert demonstrations
(described above) are drawn from this cohort.

Data Preprocessing.

The 130 continuous state features are extracted from the student–system
interaction logs across five categories: autonomy (10 features,
e.g. number of elicits since the last tell), temporal (22, e.g. average time per step), problem-solving context (31, e.g. problem
difficulty), performance (57, e.g. percentage of correct entries
so far), and hint usage (10, e.g. total hints requested).

Shared Implementation Details

Across all four datasets, MT-TICC (Section 6.5.2) uses the default
 $K=9$ temporal state clusters described in Section 6.4,
and dual-arm criticality (Section 6.5.3) uses $k=15$ nearest
neighbours with the sparse-support fallback described in
Section 6.7.
All reported results average 10 random 80/20 train/test splits, with
significance assessed by paired Wilcoxon signed-rank tests ( $p<0.05$ ), as
stated above.

## 6.6.2 Baselines

We evaluate Cchal against seven baselines, categorized into four groups by
their reward-function assumption, following the taxonomy of Yang et al. (2025b):
1) without a reward function: BC (Behavioral
Cloning) learns a direct mapping from states to actions on positive
trajectories ( $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ ), with no demonstration-quality
weighting (Gleave et al. 2022).
2) With a single reward function among all demonstrations:
GP+DQN infers per-timestamp rewards from the delayed outcome via
Gaussian Process credit assignment (Azizsoltani and Sadeghi 2018), followed
by a Deep Q-Network (Fan et al. 2020) for policy induction; and
EDM (Energy-based Distribution Matching) is a state-of-the-art
strictly offline method that assumes a single reward function across all
demonstrations (Jarrett et al. 2020a).
3) With multiple reward functions varying across
trajectories (constant within each): EM-EDM clusters demonstrations
and assigns a distinct reward function per cluster; we enhance
Babes et al. (2011) to use EDM in the M-step for continuous-state
scalability.
4) With multiple evolving reward functions: AHIRL
(Adapted Hierarchical IRL) segments trajectories via a Gaussian mixture model
and learns cluster-wise policies with maximum-entropy
IRL (Krishnan et al. 2016), adapted here to use EDM for offline policy
induction; MIL (Multi-modal Imitation Learning) jointly segments
and imitates sub-trajectory policies via an InfoGAN-style latent
intention (Hausman et al. 2017), adapted to the offline setting; and
THEMES (Yang et al. 2025b) itself, a time-aware hierarchical EM
framework with a reward-regulated MT-TICC and EM-EDM that models multiple
evolving reward functions but applies uniform within-trajectory
demonstration weighting and has no notion of decision criticality—the
direct methodological predecessor to Cchal.
Although THEMES was originally proposed as its own paper’s contribution,
here it is evaluated purely as a strong prior baseline, since Cchal is the
framework proposed in this work.

Ablations: We additionally compare Cchal against its own
ablations, each isolating one component of the joint weighting scheme:
Cchal-NoGP replaces the GP credit weights with the scalar
trajectory-level demonstration rank $q_{i}$ broadcast uniformly across
timesteps, removing within-trajectory outcome attribution while retaining
the trajectory-level rank; Cchal-NoCrit replaces both dual-arm
criticality scores with uniform weights ( $\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{hi}}(s_{t})=\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{lo}}(s_{t})=1$ 
for all $t$ ), retaining GP credit and BT contrast; and
Cchal-NoContrast removes $\mathcal{D}^{-}$ from BT-IRL, falling
back to standard MLIRL on $\mathcal{D}^{+}$ only, while retaining dual-arm
criticality and GP credit.
Finally, Cchal-Full enables all three components jointly.

## 6.6.3 Results

Table 6.2 reports F1, AUC-ROC, AUPRC, and Jaccard for
the seven baselines described above—including THEMES, now evaluated
purely as a prior hierarchical apprenticeship learning framework rather than
as the proposed method—for the full Cchal model, and for its three
ablations (Cchal-NoGP, Cchal-NoCrit, Cchal-NoContrast), all averaged
over 10 random train/test splits, with the best-performing baselines shown
in bold and the overall best results additionally marked
bold∗.
It shows that while THEMES is consistently the strongest baseline,
Cchal achieves superior performance across all four datasets and
evaluation metrics, surpassing every competing baseline and ablation.

Baseline standing.

Among the methods without a reward function or with a single reward
function, EDM performs considerably better than BC and GP+DQN on every
dataset, confirming that even a simple offline energy-based policy benefits
from some notion of demonstration quality.
Comparing EDM against the multiple-reward methods, EM-EDM improves
substantially on Mayo and MIMIC-III, is roughly comparable on ITS, and
slightly regresses on CCHS, suggesting that assuming a single,
trajectory-constant reward is not uniformly worse once trajectories are
shorter or the cohort is more homogeneous in acuity.
Among the methods with multiple evolving reward functions, THEMES
is the strongest baseline throughout—most dramatically on MIMIC-III (F1
 $0.936$ , AUC $0.935$ , Jaccard $0.883$ ), where its evolving multi-reward-function
view of the treatment trajectory has the most training signal to exploit,
and it is also the best baseline on AUC and APR for MIMIC-III and
ITS—while AHIRL and MIL, despite sharing the same evolving-reward
assumption, trail it on Mayo and CCHS, underscoring the value of
THEMES’s time-aware sub-trajectory partitioning over their coarser
segmentation heuristics.
GP+DQN and BC remain weakest throughout, consistent with their lack of any
explicit demonstration-quality weighting or hierarchical structure.

Cchal overall: best on CCHS, Mayo, and ITS across every metric; competitive with THEMES on MIMIC-III.

Cchal outperforms every measured baseline on every reported metric on CCHS
(F1 $0.675$ , AUC $0.693$ , APR $0.618$ , Jaccard $0.605$ , versus the next-best
baseline THEMES at F1 $0.668$ and Jaccard $0.517$ ), on Mayo (F1 $0.861$ ,
AUC $0.670$ , APR $0.580$ , Jaccard $0.794$ , versus THEMES at F1 $0.838$ ,
AUC $0.645$ , and Jaccard $0.744$ ), and on ITS (F1 $0.767$ , AUC $0.893$ , APR
 $0.814$ , Jaccard $0.633$ , versus THEMES, the best ITS baseline, at F1
 $0.755$ , AUC $0.868$ , APR $0.765$ , and Jaccard $0.617$ ).
On MIMIC-III, Cchal is essentially tied with THEMES on F1 ( $0.935$ vs. $0.936$ ) and AUC ( $0.934$ vs. $0.935$ )—differences well within one
reported standard deviation—while still exceeding THEMES on APR
( $0.915$ vs. $0.908$ ) and Jaccard ( $0.890$ vs. $0.883$ ); we argue in
Section 6.7 that this narrower margin reflects the
MIMIC-III cohort’s uniformly high acuity rather than a weakness of
Cchal’s mechanism.
Taken together, Cchal outright beats every baseline on every metric on
three of the four datasets (CCHS, Mayo, ITS), and on the fourth (MIMIC-III)
trades a statistically negligible F1/AUC gap against THEMES for a clear
APR/Jaccard edge—it is never meaningfully worse than the strongest prior
method anywhere in Table 6.2.
The consistent win on ITS suggests the outcome-attribution-plus-criticality
principle is not specific to septic shock quadrants—the same joint
weighting mechanism transfers to a QLG-defined quadrant structure in an
entirely different, non-clinical decision-making domain.
Section 6.6.4 attributes these gains to GP credit,
dual-arm criticality, and BT pairwise contrast individually.
