## 6.6.4 Ablation Analysis

Table 6.2 (lower block) decomposes Cchal’s gains by
component.
Cchal-Full matches or exceeds all three ablations on every metric across
all four datasets: no ablation ever outperforms Full, confirming that GP
credit, dual-arm criticality, and BT pairwise contrast are all genuinely
load-bearing rather than redundant.
The magnitude of each component’s contribution varies sharply by
dataset—removing any single component costs an average of $31\%$ relative
Jaccard on Mayo, $23\%$ on CCHS, $13\%$ on MIMIC-III, but under $1\%$ on ITS,
where Cchal-NoCrit ties Full exactly on AUC ( $0.893$ ) and Jaccard
( $0.633$ )—and this tracks trajectory length: Mayo’s mean trajectory (65
steps) is the longest of the three clinical datasets, CCHS’s (34 steps) is
shorter, and ITS’s is fixed at just 10 decisions per student.
The more decisions a trajectory contains, the more there is for GP credit and
criticality to differentiate, and the more pairs BT-IRL has to contrast; with
only 10 fixed decisions per student, ITS leaves little room for any one
component to add signal beyond the other two.
A second, compounding factor is specific to ITS: it is the same dataset on
which the underlying demonstration-rank mechanism was already validated by
Halide (Islam et al. 2026), so GP credit alone starts from a
comparatively well-calibrated rank there, leaving criticality less additional
headroom to close than in the clinical datasets, where no such prior
calibration exists.
Within each dataset, the three components contribute comparably—no single
one dominates the other two, though their relative ranking shifts slightly
across datasets (Section 6.7 returns to this point).

GP credit vs. trajectory-level quality (Cchal-NoGP).

Replacing GP credit with the uniform trajectory-level demonstration rank
costs $29.7\%$ relative Jaccard on Mayo ( $0.794\!\to\!0.558$ ), $22.8\%$ on
CCHS ( $0.605\!\to\!0.467$ ), $13.9\%$ on MIMIC-III ( $0.890\!\to\!0.766$ ), and
 $0.5\%$ on ITS ( $0.633\!\to\!0.630$ )—confirming that within-trajectory
outcome attribution, not just trajectory-level ranking, is essential
wherever trajectories are long enough to contain meaningfully different
decisions.

Dual-arm contrastive criticality (Cchal-NoCrit).

Replacing both criticality arms with uniform weights costs $31.4\%$ relative
Jaccard on Mayo ( $0.794\!\to\!0.545$ ), $22.6\%$ on CCHS ( $0.605\!\to\!0.468$ ),
 $13.6\%$ on MIMIC-III ( $0.890\!\to\!0.769$ ), and exactly $0\%$ on ITS
( $0.633\!\to\!0.633$ )—comparable in magnitude to GP credit’s contribution
on every dataset, indicating that critical-state identification provides a
selective learning signal beyond what uniform GP-credit weighting alone
captures, except where the fixed 10-step ITS horizon leaves no
within-trajectory contrast to exploit.

BT pairwise contrast (Cchal-NoContrast).

Removing $\mathcal{D}^{-}$ from BT-IRL (reverting to standard MLIRL on
 $\mathcal{D}^{+}$ ) costs $32.0\%$ relative Jaccard on Mayo
( $0.794\!\to\!0.540$ ), $22.6\%$ on CCHS ( $0.605\!\to\!0.468$ ), $12.8\%$ on
MIMIC-III ( $0.890\!\to\!0.776$ ), and $1.6\%$ on ITS ( $0.633\!\to\!0.623$ )—the
largest of the three ablation effects on Mayo and ITS, indicating that the
pairwise contrastive signal from observed failure trajectories is at least
as important as, and on some datasets more important than, GP credit and
criticality alone.

## 6.6.5 Early Sepsis Intervention & A Case Study

Figure 6.2: 
A Case Study Comparing Cchal vs. THEMES vs. EDM.
The top three panels present temperature, respiratory rate, and heart
rate, where the green regions denote normal values and the light-red
regions denote abnormal values. The fourth panel compares all treatment
actions suggested by the expert, EDM, THEMES, and Cchal.
The dashed box is magnified in the bottom panel to show the actions
preceding the expert’s first intervention.

Figure 6.2 presents a case study from CCHS. The x-axis
represents the time elapsed since patient arrival in minutes. The top three
panels illustrate changes in three important vital signs—temperature,
respiratory rate, and heart rate—in their original clinical units. The
green regions indicate normal ranges, whereas the light-red regions indicate
abnormal ranges. The fourth panel compares the actions recommended by EDM
(red triangles), THEMES (blue crosses), and Cchal (green diamonds)
against the expert actions (gray circles). The dashed box highlights the
period preceding the expert’s first intervention, which is enlarged in the
bottom panel.

As shown in the magnified window, the expert administers the first
treatment at $485.7$ minutes after arrival, versus $175.8$ minutes for EDM
( $\Delta T=309.9$ min, $\sim\!5.2$ h earlier), $91.9$ minutes for THEMES
( $\Delta T=393.8$ min, $\sim\!6.6$ h earlier), and $65.3$ minutes for
Cchal ( $\Delta T=420.4$ min, $\sim\!7.0$ h earlier)— $26.6$ minutes
earlier than THEMES and $110.5$ minutes earlier than EDM.

The patient exhibits abnormal vital-sign patterns before the expert’s
initial treatment. By assigning greater outcome credit to consequential
decisions and identifying states where successful and failed demonstrations
exhibit different action patterns, Cchal responds more promptly to these
early warning signs, while its action distribution still closely follows
the expert’s subsequent treatment pattern. This illustrates how
outcome-attributed GP credit, dual-arm criticality, and pairwise
contrastive reward learning jointly help Cchal focus policy induction on
clinically important decision points.

Finally, expert actions are used as a high-quality reference rather than a
perfect gold standard—clinical decisions are shaped by practitioner
experience, workflow, and available information, and may not represent the
earliest possible intervention. Cchal is not intended to replace
clinical judgment, but to model successful decision-making patterns and
support earlier intervention when patient states resemble critical points
observed in the demonstrations.

## 6.7 Discussion

Why the margin narrows on MIMIC-III.

Unlike CCHS and Mayo, whose cohorts are drawn from emergency-department and
general hospital admissions spanning the full acuity spectrum—from routine
monitoring to imminent shock—MIMIC-III is drawn exclusively from ICU
admissions (Johnson et al. 2016): every patient in the cohort is, by the
admission criterion itself, already critically ill.
Cchal’s dual-arm criticality mechanism (Section 6.5.3) is
designed to contrast decisions at genuinely critical junctures against
decisions at comparatively routine ones within the same risk stratum; in a
population that is uniformly high-acuity by construction, there are fewer
truly routine states left to contrast against, which compresses the
behavioral divergence the JSD criticality score is built to detect.
This is directly consistent with the ablation study
(Section 6.6.4): removing dual-arm criticality costs
 $31.4\%$ relative Jaccard on Mayo and $22.6\%$ on CCHS, but only $13.6\%$ on
MIMIC-III—the smallest of the three clinical datasets—indicating that
criticality contributes comparatively less headroom on MIMIC-III precisely
because the cohort has less within-population acuity variance for it to
exploit.
A second, compounding factor is scale: MIMIC-III’s training pool
( $|{\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}}^{\mathrm{tr}}|\approx 6{,}400$ , Section 6.7)
is roughly two orders of magnitude larger than CCHS’s or Mayo’s, giving
THEMES’s coarser trajectory-level evolving-reward mechanism enough data to
already approach a strong solution on its own, leaving less headroom for
Cchal’s more targeted decision-level refinement to improve on.
Where the cohort spans a genuine spectrum of severity (CCHS, Mayo) or the
decision space is unrelated to clinical acuity altogether (ITS), Cchal
improves over THEMES on every metric; on the one dataset where acuity is
deliberately held uniform by the inclusion criterion, Cchal still matches
THEMES on the ranking metrics (F1, AUC) and exceeds it on APR and
Jaccard, rather than underperforming—the predicted behavior of a mechanism
whose value scales with population heterogeneity, not a failure of that
mechanism.

Connection to CriticalDRL.

CriticalDRL (Ju et al. 2021a) defines critical states via Q-value
spread (LongTR) under a trained policy—a model-derived signal
requiring a converged DQN.
Cchal’s criticality is data-derived from observed behavioral
contrast, requiring no trained policy.
This is particularly advantageous in the offline clinical setting where a
reliable online DQN cannot be trained.
Moreover, CriticalDRL uses criticality to reduce decision burden
(HC-MA framework), while Cchal uses it to concentrate learning
signal, serving a fundamentally different purpose.

InferGP kernel choice.

The RBF kernel in feature-action space encodes the prior that clinically
similar $(s,a)$ configurations carry correlated per-timestep quality,
regardless of their position within a trajectory.
This is more appropriate than a temporal kernel (e.g., RBF over $t/n_{i}$ ):
clinical deterioration can occur at any point, and two patients at the same
physiological state should share credit even if their trajectories differ in
length.
A Matérn- $1/2$ kernel would be appropriate for sharper
feature-space transitions; preliminary experiments showed RBF slightly
outperformed it, consistent with the relatively smooth physiological
trajectories in our data.

Limitations.

The dual-arm kNN criticality requires sufficient neighbours from both pools
in each arm: $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ / $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ for the high-risk arm and
 $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ / $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}}$ for the low-risk arm.
On small datasets (e.g., CCHS with $|{\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}}^{\mathrm{tr}}|\approx 96$ ),
under-supported feature-space regions could in principle produce noisy JSD
estimates that compress the criticality signal toward noise; the ablation
study (Section 6.6.4) does not show this manifesting as
a regression—Cchal-Full matches or exceeds Cchal-NoCrit on every
CCHS and Mayo metric—but the underlying sensitivity to pool size remains a
design concern as datasets shrink further, and is most visible on ITS, whose
fixed 10-step horizon leaves too few within-trajectory contrasts for
criticality to add signal beyond GP credit and BT contrast.
Adaptive $k$ selection—scaling $k$ with pool size rather than fixing
 $k=15$ —or cluster-level JSD (using MT-TICC assignments instead of
raw-feature kNN) would give more reliable criticality estimates without
per-dataset tuning.
The low-risk arm is inactive when only $\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ / $\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ 
data are available (DATA_MODE=e), degrading gracefully to single-arm
behavior.
The InferGP credit assignment is computed offline on the full training set
in feature-action space; distributional shift between the full dataset and
any particular train/test split is not accounted for in the kernel.
Per-fold GP inference would eliminate this, at higher computational cost.

## 6.8 Conclusion

We proposed Cchal, a framework for apprenticeship learning from
imperfect clinical demonstrations that directs learning attention to the
decisions that matter most: those that contributed to the trajectory’s
outcome and that distinguish expert from non-expert clinical
behavior.
By combining outcome-attributed decision weighting via GP inference with
data-driven contrastive criticality scoring and a Bradley-Terry pairwise IRL
objective, Cchal addresses the joint challenge of demonstration quality
variation and within-trajectory decision importance heterogeneity—without
requiring a trained policy or environment reward signal.
Empirical results on three EHR datasets demonstrate consistent improvements
over THEMES and other published baselines, with ablations confirming that
outcome attribution, criticality scoring, and pairwise contrastive IRL each
contribute independently to this gain.
Cchal also outperforms every baseline on a fourth, non-clinical ITS
dataset for student modeling, providing initial empirical evidence that the
core principle—that decision importance in apprenticeship learning has two
orthogonal dimensions (outcome attribution and behavioral criticality), and
that both are needed to concentrate learning where it matters—generalizes
beyond clinical EHR to any offline imitation learning setting with access to
both successful and failed demonstrations.

## Chapter 7 Empirical Evaluation of Offline AL in Classroom Study

## 7.1 Overview

Intelligent Tutoring Systems (ITSs) can improve student learning by adaptively guiding instructional activity selection, yet designing effective pedagogical policies without requiring costly or unethical live experimentation remains an open challenge. We present StuEDM, a student-choice-with-override pedagogical policy induced via Energy-based Distribution Matching (EDM) (Jarrett et al. 2020a), a strictly batch offline apprenticeship learning algorithm that reproduces expert tutoring behavior by matching state occupancy distributions—without a reward function or environment interaction. In StuEDM, students freely choose their presentation mode (Problem Solving, Worked Example, or Collaborative Problem Solving) for each training problem; the EDM-induced policy intervenes only when a student’s choice conflicts with the EDM-derived policy, and does so using demonstrations drawn only from sessions in which students exercised their own pedagogical agency—unlike RL corpora, which are commonly assembled from tutor- or randomly-driven exploratory logs rather than students’ own choices—a substantially smaller, semester-limited corpus than the large historical interaction logs available to reward-optimizing RL. We compare StuEDM against StuCQL—an analogous student-choice-with-override policy using Conservative Q-Learning trained on a large corpus of interaction data—and Expert, an uninformed baseline that assigns among the same domain-expert-designed presentation modes with no adaptive or student-driven guidance, in a controlled classroom study with 104 students. All three conditions produced significant pre-to-post learning gains (partial $\eta^{2}=0.29$ – $0.69$ ).

Critically, StuEDM yielded the highest normalized learning gains across all five outcome measures: planned one-sided comparisons showed StuEDM significantly outperformed a size-matched, pretest-equivalent Expert baseline on four of five measures (pretest-adjusted posttest $p=0.017$ , normalized learning gain $p=0.016$ , isomorphic normalized learning gain $p=0.038$ , score-ratio normalized learning gain $p=0.015$ ); significantly outperformed StuCQL on the pretest-adjusted posttest ( $p=0.048$ ), with the remaining direct StuCQL comparisons trending in the same direction but not reaching significance ( $p=0.067$ – $0.117$ ); and outperformed the combined comparison conditions on four of five measures ( $p=0.026$ – $0.045$ ).

StuEDM’s advantage over Expert is thus the more robust finding, while its advantage over StuCQL is directionally consistent but weaker. StuEDM students also engaged with the tutor significantly longer than StuCQL students ( $p=0.008$ ), a further engagement-related outcome favoring StuEDM; EDM itself was trained on a substantially smaller student-choice demonstration corpus than the reward-optimizing RL policy it is compared against. A decision-level behavioral analysis clarifies a further mechanism: StuEDM overrode students’ own choices on only 28.2% of decisions, versus 66.7% for StuCQL ( $\chi^{2}(1)=114.15$ , $p<.001$ ), and its overrides steered students toward independent Problem Solving while preserving some presentation-mode diversity, whereas StuCQL’s overrides converged almost entirely on Worked Examples and never once selected Collaborative Problem Solving.

## 7.2 Introduction

Intelligent Tutoring Systems have demonstrated consistent learning benefits across educational domains by providing individualized, on-demand instruction (VanLehn 2011; Vanlehn 2006). A core challenge in ITS design is the pedagogical policy: the mechanism that selects which instructional activity a student should engage with next given their current knowledge state. Poor sequencing wastes limited study time and can lead to disengagement, while well-designed policies have been shown to substantially increase learning efficiency (Corbett and Anderson 1994; Ausin et al. 2020a; Abdelshiheed et al. 2023b).

Data-driven policy induction from historical student logs has emerged as a principled approach that scales to complex student behavior. One class of policies preserves student autonomy by allowing students to make their own pedagogical decisions—choosing, for instance, between Problem Solving, Worked Examples, and Collaborative Problem Solving—while overriding those decisions only in cases of disagreement with a learned policy. Such student-choice-with-override policies (Hostetter et al. 2023b) are an instance of mixed-initiative interaction (Horvitz 1999), in which control over an action is shared between a user and an automated agent rather than assigned entirely to one or the other; here they balance student agency with data-driven guidance, and have been shown to outperform fully instructor-controlled policies (Ausin et al. 2020a; Hostetter et al. 2023b). Empirical ITS research further shows that student agency can increase engagement and learning efficiency, but learners vary widely in the effectiveness of their autonomous decisions, motivating selective intervention (Ju et al. 2022; Hostetter et al. 2025).

Reinforcement Learning (RL) methods—including deep Q-networks and Conservative Q-Learning (CQL) (Kumar et al. 2020)—frame policy learning as reward maximization over student interaction trajectories, typically using a delayed learning gain signal as the reward (Zhou et al. 2019; Ausin et al. 2020a; Abdelshiheed et al. 2023b; Hostetter et al. 2023b). While effective, reward-based approaches face three well-known limitations in the ITS setting. First, specifying a reward function that fully captures the goals of instruction—including learning, transfer, engagement, and efficiency—is non-trivial. Second, offline RL methods such as CQL require large corpora of student interaction data and can still generalize poorly to student states that are underrepresented in the training distribution, even with conservative regularization (Kumar et al. 2020; Hostetter et al. 2023b). Third, and central to student agency, a prevalent practice for assembling this training corpus is to first deploy a cohort of students on an ITS that makes random, yet pedagogically reasonable, decisions, generating an exploratory corpus from which a policy is subsequently derived (Islam et al. 2024); the resulting policy is therefore learned from what the tutor—or a random-assignment mechanism—decided at each step, not from what students themselves chose to do. Despite this practice, a substantial portion of prior work derives pedagogical policies from fewer than 3,000 student–tutor interaction logs, and the resulting policies can still prove ineffective (Islam et al. 2024).

Apprenticeship learning offers an alternative: rather than optimizing a reward, it learns to reproduce expert behavior from demonstrations (Abbeel and Ng 2004; Pomerleau 1991). Classical AL assumes demonstrations are generated by a uniformly expert demonstrator, an assumption that rarely holds for real human behavior—including students’ own pedagogical decisions, which range from highly effective to counterproductive. Rather than hand-curating an expert-only subset or treating every demonstration as equally authoritative, recent work addresses this by continuously weighting each demonstration’s influence by its estimated expertise (Islam et al. 2026), an approach StuEDM adopts (Section 7.4). Classical approaches such as behavioral cloning suffer from compounding errors under distributional shift (Ross et al. 2011b), and most modern apprenticeship learning algorithms require interactive environment access to correct such errors. Strictly offline apprenticeship learning—operating on a fixed dataset with no environment interaction—remains challenging (Jarrett et al. 2020a). Jarrett et al. (Jarrett et al. 2020a) proposed Energy-based Distribution Matching (EDM), a strictly offline apprenticeship learning algorithm that learns a policy by matching the state occupancy distribution of expert demonstrations using an energy-based model, avoiding both reward engineering and online rollouts. EDM is particularly well-suited to educational settings, where live policy experimentation raises ethical and logistical concerns, expert demonstrations—historical logs of effective tutoring interactions—are available in limited quantities, and reward specification is difficult. Critically, EDM requires only a small set of expert demonstrations, making it feasible in domains where large-scale interaction logs do not exist.

In this chapter we present StuEDM, a student-choice-with-override pedagogical policy for the Pyrenees probability ITS (Ausin et al. 2020a; Hostetter et al. 2023c) in which students freely choose their presentation mode and EDM intervenes only when their choice conflicts with the expert-derived policy. We compare it in a three-condition classroom study ( $N=104$ ) against StuCQL—an analogous student-choice-with-override policy whose override logic is provided by CQL trained on a large interaction corpus—and Expert, an uninformed baseline assigned uniformly among the same domain-expert-designed presentation modes. We ask:

• 

RQ1 (Learning Outcomes): Does an EDM-induced student-choice-with-override policy, trained on a substantially smaller student-choice demonstration corpus, yield greater normalized learning gains than an analogous CQL-induced policy trained on a much larger interaction corpus?

• 

RQ2 (Student Agency): How do StuCQL and StuEDM differ in how often they override a student’s own pedagogical choice and in which presentation mode they steer students toward when they do, and does training on students’ own decisions—rather than on tutor- or randomly-driven logs—translate into a policy that better preserves student agency in deployment?

Our main contributions are:

1. 

The first application of energy-based distribution matching to student-choice-with-override pedagogical policy induction in a deployed ITS, trained on a substantially smaller student-choice demonstration corpus than typical offline-RL pedagogical policies—and, because apprenticeship learning can only imitate decisions the student actually made, one whose training data is inherently agency-driven, unlike RL corpora commonly assembled from tutor- or randomly-driven exploratory logs.

2. 

A controlled classroom study, with Expert’s underpowered analytic sample augmented by a pretest-equivalent prior-semester cohort (Section 7.5.2) to match StuCQL’s and StuEDM’s sample sizes, demonstrating that StuEDM yields the highest normalized learning gains across all five outcome measures, with planned directional comparisons reaching significance against Expert on four of five measures (pretest-adjusted posttest $p=0.017$ , NLG_post $p=0.016$ , NLG_post_iso $p=0.038$ , NLG_post_SR $p=0.015$ ), against StuCQL on the pretest-adjusted posttest only ( $p=0.048$ , with the remaining comparisons trending the same direction at $p=0.067$ – $0.117$ ), and against the combined baseline on four of five measures ( $p=0.026$ – $0.045$ ).

3. 

Evidence that EDM-based offline apprenticeship learning from a substantially smaller student-choice corpus outperforms CQL trained on a much larger, longer-span interaction corpus, along with a transparent accounting of where that advantage does and does not hold: it is not attributable to additional training-corpus size. StuEDM students additionally engaged with the tutor significantly longer than StuCQL students, an engagement outcome we report alongside the learning-gain measures rather than treating time on task only as a covariate to control away.

4. 

A decision-level behavioral analysis showing why the two policies diverge in outcomes: StuEDM overrides students less than half as often as StuCQL (28.2% vs. 66.7% of decisions) and, when it does override, steers students toward independent Problem Solving while still preserving some diversity of presentation modes, whereas StuCQL’s overrides converge almost entirely on Worked Examples and never once select Collaborative Problem Solving.

## 7.3 Background and Related Work

## 7.3.1 Student Agency and Mixed-Initiative ITS Policies

Student agency—the extent to which learners can direct their own instructional experience—has long been studied as a determinant of engagement, motivation, and learning outcomes, though its effects are mixed: greater learner control over pacing, sequencing, or content can improve motivation and engagement, but is not reliably associated with better objective performance, and learners with weak self-regulation skills can be overwhelmed by too much autonomy (Gupta et al. 2024). In ITS design specifically, autonomy affordance interacts with learner characteristics rather than uniformly helping or harming outcomes (Ausin et al. 2020a). Mixed-initiative interaction (Horvitz 1999), in which control over an action is shared between a human and an automated agent, offers a middle ground between full learner control and full system control. Student-choice-with-override policies apply this paradigm to pedagogical sequencing: a student proposes a presentation-mode decision, and a learned policy intervenes only on disagreement (Hostetter et al. 2023b; Ju et al. 2022). This design has been shown to outperform both pure student choice and fully system-controlled sequencing (Ausin et al. 2020a; Hostetter et al. 2023b), but it raises a question orthogonal to learning outcomes: how much of the student’s agency does the resulting policy actually preserve in deployment? Override rate and delivered-mode diversity are natural, directly measurable proxies for this, yet prior student-choice-with-override work has evaluated such policies primarily on learning gain, without characterizing how often, or toward which mode, the induced policy overrides the student. Recent CHI work on adaptive systems more broadly finds that users consistently want transparency into, and control over, the personalization applied to them, not just more accurate personalization (Wu et al. 2026), reinforcing that override behavior is a first-class design concern in its own right. We return to this question directly as RQ2, and additionally ask whether the source of the training data itself—student-made decisions versus tutor- or randomly-assigned ones—affects how agency-preserving the resulting policy is.

## 7.3.2 Offline Reinforcement Learning for ITS Policy Induction

Sequential pedagogical decisions naturally map to a Markov Decision Process: an agent observes the student’s learning state, selects an instructional action (e.g., problem type or presentation mode), and receives a delayed reward reflecting the resulting learning gain. Offline RL methods learn policies from pre-collected interaction logs without interacting with the environment during training, making them suitable for educational deployments where live A/B testing is constrained.

Prior work has applied deep Q-networks, hierarchical RL, and actor-critic methods to ITS policy induction (Zhou et al. 2019; Ju et al. 2021b; Ausin et al. 2019; Abdelshiheed et al. 2023b; Hostetter et al. 2023b). Zhou et al. (Zhou et al. 2019) used hierarchical RL on a logic tutor, significantly outperforming expert and random baselines. Sanz Ausin et al. (Ausin et al. 2019) applied batch deep RL to problem-level sequencing on Pyrenees, finding that the RL policy with simple explanations outperformed an expert policy. Abdelshiheed et al. (Abdelshiheed et al. 2023b) demonstrated that deep RL-based adaptive metacognitive interventions can close skill gaps between student subgroups and support preparation for future learning.

A key limitation of offline RL is distributional shift: the learned Q-function can be arbitrarily overoptimistic on state-action pairs not well-covered by the training data, leading to unsafe extrapolation (Kumar et al. 2020). CQL (Kumar et al. 2020) addresses this by adding a conservative regularization term that penalizes high Q-values outside the data distribution, but still requires a large corpus of interaction data to estimate Q-values reliably. Hostetter et al. (Hostetter et al. 2023b) applied CQL to Pyrenees as a student-choice-with-override policy (StuCQL): students freely select their presentation mode (PS, WE, or CPS) and the CQL-induced policy overrides only when it disagrees with the student’s choice. This design respects student autonomy while providing data-driven guidance, and was shown to significantly outperform pure student-choice and random baselines. The same work also used fuzzy logic to distill the RL-induced policy into interpretable IF-THEN rules (Hostetter et al. 2023b).

## 7.3.3 Apprenticeship Learning and Energy-based Models

Apprenticeship learning aims to recover an expert’s policy from demonstrations alone, without a reward signal (Abbeel and Ng 2004; Pomerleau 1991). Behavioral cloning treats this as supervised classification but suffers from compounding distributional errors at deployment. More recent methods such as GAIL (Ho and Ermon 2016b) and AIRL (Fu et al. 2018) frame apprenticeship learning as adversarial optimization, achieving near-expert performance but requiring online policy rollouts during training—infeasible in strictly offline educational settings.

Jarrett et al. (Jarrett et al. 2020a) proposed Energy-based Distribution Matching (EDM) to address strictly offline apprenticeship learning. The key insight is to identify the discriminator between expert and learner state occupancies with an energy-based model of the expert state distribution. Concretely, given a dataset of expert trajectories $\mathcal{D}=\{(s_{t},a_{t})\}$ , EDM minimizes the KL divergence between the learner’s state occupancy measure $\rho_{\pi}$ and the expert’s $\rho_{E}$ :

 $\min_{\pi}\;D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\left(\rho_{E}\,\|\,\rho_{\pi}\right)$ 

using an energy function $E_{\theta}(s)$ that models the log-density of the expert state distribution, avoiding the need to roll out $\pi$ in the environment. This makes EDM uniquely suited to settings where only a static log of interactions is available and no simulator exists.

Prior work has applied apprenticeship learning directly to student interaction data from the same Pyrenees platform (Islam et al. 2024; Islam et al. 2025): rather than gathering exploratory data as in DRL, these methods derive pedagogical policies directly from the decisions and behaviors students themselves demonstrated while interacting with the ITS, treating students’ pedagogical choices as demonstrations and inducing policies that model observed decision-making behavior. Yang et al. (Yang et al. 2025a) extended this with Themes, a hierarchical apprenticeship learning framework that partitions student trajectories into temporally coherent sub-trajectories and induces cluster-specific policies to capture evolving pedagogical strategies. These works establish apprenticeship learning from student choice data as a viable alternative to reward-optimizing RL on this platform. Because apprenticeship learning imitates rather than optimizes, it can only be trained on interactions in which the demonstrator—here, the student—actually made the pedagogical decision, in contrast to RL corpora, which are commonly tutor- or randomly-driven (Section 7.3.1); the present work extends this line of research by deploying the induced policy as a student-choice-with-override intervention in a live classroom study and directly evaluating how well it preserves student agency in deployment.

## 7.3.4 ITS Policy Evaluation in the Classroom

Live classroom evaluations of ITS policies face well-documented statistical challenges: per-condition sample sizes are modest, pre-existing knowledge varies widely, and effect sizes for between-condition differences are typically small even when within-condition gains are large (VanLehn 2011; Chi et al. 2011b). Standard practices include: excluding ceiling performers from inferential analyses; verifying pretest equivalence; using Normalized Learning Gain (NLG) to control for incoming competence (Chi et al. 2011b); and administering isomorphic posttests to assess transfer (Ausin et al. 2019; Abdelshiheed et al. 2023b; Hostetter et al. 2023c). Hostetter et al. (Hostetter et al. 2023c) further showed that delivering real-time personalized explanations alongside RL-induced interventions significantly improves student NLG on Pyrenees. We follow all of these conventions. A complementary, instructional-design route to the same differentiation goal has recently appeared in the CHI literature: rather than inducing a policy over an existing set of presentation modes, Dey Tithi et al. (Dey Tithi et al. 2026) designed new cognitively-engaging worked-example variants for a logic tutor and showed their effectiveness varies systematically with students’ prior knowledge. Our work instead holds the presentation modes fixed and asks how a policy should be induced to select among them, but both lines of work point to the same underlying HCI problem: matching the level and kind of instructional support to a learner’s individual differences.

## 7.4 System and Policies

## 7.4.1 Pyrenees: A Probability ITS

Pyrenees is a web-based ITS that teaches ten probability principles (e.g., the Complement Theorem and Bayes’ Theorem) (Ausin et al. 2020a; Hostetter et al. 2023c). During training, students work through twelve problems and for each problem choose a presentation mode: Problem Solving (PS, independent), Worked Example (WE, tutor-presented), or Collaborative Problem Solving (CPS, co-constructed). Students proceed step-by-step—defining variables, applying probability principles, and solving equations—with immediate step-level feedback and on-demand hints. All assessments are delivered without hints or materials access, double-blind graded using a partial-credit rubric, and normalized to $[0,1]$ .

Assessment. The pretest contains 8 open-ended probability problems and the standard posttest contains 12. Both are administered without hints or access to materials, independently graded by two evaluators using a double-blind process with discrepancies resolved by discussion, and scores are normalized to $[0,1]$ . An isomorphic posttest (post_iso) contains structurally equivalent but surface-novel problems to assess transfer.

Learning outcome metrics. We report two normalized learning gain variants. The primary measure NLG_post uses Hake’s formula (Chi et al. 2011b):

 $\texttt{NLG}=\frac{\mathit{post}-\mathit{pre}}{1-\mathit{pre}}$ 

A second variant NLG_post_SR uses the square-root denominator adopted by prior work on this platform (Ausin et al. 2019; Hostetter et al. 2023c):

 $\texttt{NLG\_SR}=\frac{\mathit{post}-\mathit{pre}}{\sqrt{1-\mathit{pre}}}$ 

Both measures quantify learning efficiency independent of incoming competence. Analogous measures NLG_post_iso and NLG_post_iso_SR apply the same formulas to the isomorphic posttest.

## 7.4.2 Experimental Conditions

All three conditions deployed on the same Pyrenees platform with the same content and feedback mechanisms; they differed only in how the presentation mode for each training problem was determined. StuCQL and StuEDM are student-choice-with-override policies: the student first selects a presentation mode (PS, WE, or CPS), and the policy accepts that choice if it agrees; otherwise the policy overrides it with its own recommendation. Expert provides a fully uninformed control in which no student input is solicited.

Expert. The presentation mode is assigned uniformly at random from $\{\mathrm{PS},\mathrm{WE},\mathrm{CPS}\}$ , independent of the student’s state or preferences; because all three modes are themselves domain-expert-designed instructional strategies, this condition delivers only expert-authored content without any adaptive or student-driven guidance selecting among them. We adopt the Expert label used for the analogous non-adaptive, uniformly-assigned baseline in prior work on this platform (Ausin et al. 2019). This uncontrolled baseline captures the inherent learning potential of Pyrenees content independent of sequencing policy.

StuCQL. Students freely choose their presentation mode for each problem. Their choice is honored unless it conflicts with the policy induced by Conservative Q-Learning (CQL) (Kumar et al. 2020); when there is a conflict, the CQL-induced policy overrides the student’s selection. CQL was trained offline on 2,716 students’ interaction logs collected over 13 semesters of classroom deployments (Fall 2015–Spring 2022), using NLG as the reward signal (Islam et al. 2024). Because CQL is reward-optimizing rather than imitation-based, its training corpus can draw on any historical interaction log regardless of whether the tutor or the student made the pedagogical decision at each step. The state representation comprises 130 continuous features capturing five categories of student behavior: 57 performance features (e.g., percentage of correct responses), 22 temporal features (e.g., average time per step), 31 problem-solving features (e.g., difficulty level), 10 hint-usage features (e.g., total hints requested), and 10 autonomy features (e.g., number of elicits since the last tell) (Abdelshiheed et al. 2023b; Hostetter et al. 2023b; Islam et al. 2024). CQL’s conservative regularization penalizes high Q-values for state-action pairs underrepresented in the training data, reducing overestimation under distributional shift.

StuEDM. Students freely choose their presentation mode for each problem. Their choice is honored unless it conflicts with the policy induced by Energy-based Distribution Matching (EDM) (Jarrett et al. 2020a); when there is a conflict, the EDM-induced policy overrides the student’s selection. In contrast to StuCQL, EDM is an offline apprenticeship learning algorithm that is trained to imitate demonstrated behavior rather than to optimize a reward, and so it can only be trained on interactions in which the demonstrator—here, the student—actually made the pedagogical decision. This yields a substantially smaller and shorter-span training corpus than CQL’s: EDM was trained on the complete interaction trajectories of 244 students across five semesters in which students exercised free presentation-mode choice (S21, S22, S24, F24, and S25; 57, 56, 54, 44, and 33 students, respectively), using the same 130-dimensional state representation and 3-way action space as StuCQL. Rather than optimizing a reward, EDM learns a policy whose state occupancy distribution matches that of the demonstrations. The energy function $E_{\theta}(s)$ is parameterized by a neural network trained to assign low energy to states frequently visited in high-quality trajectories and high energy to rarely visited or low-quality-associated states; the resulting policy steers students toward high-density, high-quality states without reward engineering or online rollouts. Because real student demonstrations are rarely uniformly expert, each demonstrated decision is weighted by its estimated expertise—a sigmoid transform of standardized Normalized Learning Gain, $w=\sigma(\alpha\cdot\mathrm{NLG}_{z})$ with $\alpha=3.0$ —following the expertise-weighting scheme introduced by Halide (Islam et al. 2026): demonstrations from stronger performers receive proportionally more influence during policy induction, while weaker performers’ decisions are down-weighted rather than discarded outright, so the induced policy is shaped predominantly by expert-like behavior without requiring a hand-curated expert-only subset. The resulting policy network is a 2-hidden-layer multilayer perceptron (256 units per layer, ELU activations) mapping the 130-dimensional state to the 3 action logits, trained for 100 update steps with batch size 32, using stochastic gradient Langevin dynamics for energy-model sampling (replay buffer size 10,000, SGLD learning rate 0.01).

## 7.5 Experimental Setup

## 7.5.1 Participants

This study evaluated three experimental conditions: Expert (condition 26110), StuCQL (condition 26140), and StuEDM (condition 26150). A total of 104 students participated: Expert ( $N=26$ ), StuCQL ( $N=39$ ), and StuEDM ( $N=39$ ). Expert was allocated fewer students than StuCQL or StuEDM by original design (28 assigned vs. 43 for each of the other two conditions). Students with perfect pretest scores (pre $=1.0$ ) were excluded from inferential analyses to avoid ceiling effects on the learning gain metrics (Ausin et al. 2020a; Hostetter et al. 2023c), yielding final analytic samples of StuCQL ( $N=27$ ) and StuEDM ( $N=26$ ); the resulting Expert analytic sample ( $N=16$ ) is correspondingly underpowered, which we address next.

## 7.5.2 Expert Condition Augmentation

To bring Expert’s analytic sample to a comparable size, we supplemented it with students from the immediately preceding offering of the same course on the same platform (Spring 2025), which included an identically implemented Expert condition: uniform assignment among PS/WE/CPS with no student input, the same 12 training problems, and the same pretest/posttest instruments. Before pooling, we verified the two semesters’ Expert populations did not differ on incoming competence: pretest scores were statistically equivalent ( $t(49)=1.027$ , $p=0.310$ ; Mann-Whitney $p=0.251$ ), as was time on task ( $t(49)=0.199$ , $p=0.843$ ). From the pool of 35 pretest-non-ceiling Spring 2025 Expert students, we drew, with a fixed random seed (1234), exactly the 11 students needed to bring Expert’s analytic sample to $N=27$ , matching StuCQL. All primary analyses below use this augmented Expert sample ( $N=27$ , $N=27$ , $N=26$ across Expert, StuCQL, StuEDM). We report the augmented comparison as the primary analysis given the strength of the equivalence checks, but note that StuCQL and StuEDM reflect only the Spring 2026 cohort: no comparably designed prior-semester condition exists for either, since the closest Spring 2025 analogs paired the CQL-induced override with real-time personalized explanations (Hostetter et al. 2023c) rather than the plain CQL- or EDM-induced override used here.

## 7.5.3 Procedure

Pyrenees was administered as a homework assignment in an undergraduate Computer Science course at a university during Spring 2026. Students were randomly assigned to conditions and told that full credit required completion; grading was based on demonstrated effort rather than performance (Ausin et al. 2020a; Hostetter et al. 2023c). The experiment followed four stages:

1. 

Textbook: Students read an online textbook introducing the ten probability principles with examples.

2. 

Pretest: Students solved probability problems independently without hints, feedback, or materials access.

3. 

ITS Training: Students worked through twelve probability problems in Pyrenees under their assigned pedagogical policy. In the StuCQL and StuEDM conditions, students selected their preferred presentation mode for each problem; the respective policy intervened only when the student’s choice conflicted with the induced policy. In the Expert condition, presentation modes were assigned randomly among the expert-designed activity types with no student input. All other system behavior (hints, step-level feedback) was identical across conditions.

4. 

Posttest: Students completed the standard and isomorphic posttests under the same conditions as the pretest.

## 7.5.4 Completion Rates

Completion rates did not significantly differ across conditions. Relative to all assigned students: $92.9\%$ ( $26/28$ ) for Expert, $90.7\%$ ( $39/43$ ) for StuCQL, and $90.7\%$ ( $39/43$ ) for StuEDM, $\chi^{2}(2)=0.123$ , $p=0.940$ . Relative to students who started the tutor: $92.9\%$ ( $26/28$ ), $95.1\%$ ( $39/41$ ), and $97.5\%$ ( $39/40$ ) respectively, $\chi^{2}(2)=0.824$ , $p=0.662$ . Differential attrition is therefore unlikely to explain any observed performance differences.

## 7.6 Results

Sections 7.6.1–7.6.3 and 7.6.5 address RQ1 (learning outcomes); Section 7.6.4 addresses RQ2 (the decision-level mechanism behind those outcomes).

## 7.6.1 Pretest Equivalence

Pretest scores did not significantly differ across conditions: one-way ANOVA, $F(2,77)=0.207$ , $p=0.814$ . A Kruskal-Wallis robustness check yielded the same conclusion, $H(2)=1.054$ , $p=0.597$ , indicating balanced incoming competence across all three conditions, including the augmented Expert sample. Having established that all three groups entered the study with equivalent incoming competence, we treat posttest performance, normalized learning gains, and time on task as the outcome measures that may legitimately differ across conditions (Sections 7.6.2–7.6.3).

## 7.6.2 Within-Condition Learning Gains

We assessed within-condition learning using repeated one-way ANOVAs comparing pretest with posttest scores (Table 7.1).

Standard posttest. All three conditions showed significant pre-to-post gains: Expert, $F(1,26)=11.058$ , $p=0.003$ , partial $\eta^{2}=0.298$ ; StuCQL, $F(1,26)=28.778$ , $p<0.0005$ , partial $\eta^{2}=0.525$ ; StuEDM, $F(1,25)=31.991$ , $p<0.0005$ , partial $\eta^{2}=0.561$ .

Isomorphic posttest. The same pattern held for post_iso: Expert, $F(1,26)=23.691$ , $p<0.0005$ , partial $\eta^{2}=0.477$ ; StuCQL, $F(1,26)=57.429$ , $p<0.0005$ , partial $\eta^{2}=0.688$ ; StuEDM, $F(1,25)=42.312$ , $p<0.0005$ , partial $\eta^{2}=0.629$ .

All three policies therefore supported significant learning on both the standard and isomorphic posttests, validating the effectiveness of the underlying Pyrenees platform regardless of sequencing policy. The effect sizes were largest for StuEDM and StuCQL and smallest for Expert, consistent with the hypothesis that adaptive policies produce more effective learning than uncontrolled sequencing.

## 7.6.3 Between-Condition Comparisons

Table 7.1 summarizes mean learning outcomes. Following prior work on this platform, we test planned one-sided directional hypotheses that StuEDM outperforms the comparison conditions (Ausin et al. 2020a; Hostetter et al. 2023c).

Standard posttest (ANCOVA). Controlling for pretest, the condition effect was not significant, $F(2,76)=2.560$ , $p=0.084$ , partial $\eta^{2}=0.063$ . StuEDM produced the highest adjusted posttest numerically. Planned one-sided contrasts on the pretest-adjusted model showed StuEDM significantly outperformed both StuCQL ( $t(76)=1.690$ , $p=0.048$ ) and Expert ( $t(76)=2.154$ , $p=0.017$ ); a planned one-sided contrast of StuEDM against the (unadjusted) average of Expert and StuCQL approached significance, $t(77)=1.656$ , one-sided $p=0.051$ .

Normalized learning gain (NLG_post). The ANOVA was not significant, $F(2,77)=2.387$ , $p=0.099$ , partial $\eta^{2}=0.058$ , but condition means were ordered in the expected direction: StuEDM ( $M=0.685$ ) $>$ StuCQL ( $M=0.485$ ) $>$ Expert ( $M=0.331$ ). A planned one-sided comparison showed StuEDM significantly outperformed Expert, $t(77)=2.180$ , one-sided $p=0.016$ ; the comparison with StuCQL did not reach significance, $t(77)=1.231$ , one-sided $p=0.111$ . A planned one-sided contrast against the combined baseline was significant, $t(77)=1.963$ , one-sided $p=0.027$ .

Isomorphic posttest (post_iso). The ANOVA was not significant, $F(2,77)=1.625$ , $p=0.204$ , but means favored StuEDM ( $M=0.948$ ) over StuCQL ( $M=0.888$ ) and Expert ( $M=0.885$ ). Neither pairwise comparison reached significance: StuEDM vs. StuCQL, $t(77)=1.518$ , one-sided $p=0.067$ ; StuEDM vs. Expert, $t(77)=1.611$ , one-sided $p=0.056$ . A planned one-sided contrast against the combined baseline was significant, $t(77)=1.800$ , one-sided $p=0.038$ .

Isomorphic NLG (NLG_post_iso). The ANOVA was not significant, $F(2,77)=1.664$ , $p=0.196$ , but means were ordered as expected: StuEDM ( $M=0.829$ ) $>$ StuCQL ( $M=0.676$ ) $>$ Expert ( $M=0.600$ ). A planned one-sided comparison showed StuEDM significantly outperformed Expert, $t(77)=1.795$ , one-sided $p=0.038$ ; the comparison with StuCQL did not reach significance, $t(77)=1.197$ , one-sided $p=0.117$ . A planned one-sided contrast against the combined baseline was significant, $t(77)=1.722$ , one-sided $p=0.045$ .

Score-ratio NLG (NLG_post_SR). The ANOVA was not significant, $F(2,77)=2.471$ , $p=0.091$ . StuEDM significantly outperformed Expert, $t(77)=2.221$ , one-sided $p=0.015$ , though the comparison with StuCQL did not reach significance, $t(77)=1.216$ , one-sided $p=0.114$ . A planned one-sided contrast against the combined baseline was significant, $t(77)=1.978$ , one-sided $p=0.026$ .

Time on task (engagement).

We additionally treat time spent on ITS training as an outcome measure of student engagement: within a fixed homework-completion structure where grading was based on effort rather than performance, students freely chose how long to remain engaged with the tutor, so more time on task reflects greater voluntary engagement rather than a cost to be minimized. Time on task differed significantly across conditions overall: one-way ANOVA, $F(2,77)=4.206$ , $p=0.019$ . Pairwise $t$ -tests show StuEDM students engaged with the tutor significantly longer than StuCQL students ( $M=1.52$ h vs. $M=1.11$ h; $t(77)=2.714$ , $p=0.008$ ), and StuCQL students engaged for significantly less time than Expert students ( $M=1.11$ h vs. $M=1.44$ h; $t(77)=-2.232$ , $p=0.029$ ); StuEDM and Expert did not differ ( $t(77)=0.503$ , $p=0.617$ ). The StuEDM-vs-StuCQL engagement difference is present in the S26-only data as well ( $t(51)=2.419$ , $p=0.019$ , since neither condition’s data changed under augmentation), so it is not an artifact of the Expert augmentation. StuEDM thus produced both the highest learning outcomes and the highest voluntary engagement with the tutor among the two student-choice-with-override conditions.

## 7.6.4 RQ2: Student Agency — Decision Distribution and Policy Overrides

To understand the behavioral mechanism behind these outcome differences, we analyzed all 390 problem-level presentation-mode decisions logged for StuCQL and StuEDM (39 students $\times$ 10 choice-eligible problems each), reconstructing, for every decision, the student’s own stated choice and the policy’s applied (post-override) decision among Problem Solving (PS), Worked Example (WE), and Collaborative Problem Solving (CPS).

Students’ own choices are equivalent at the first decision but diverge over the remaining nine. Because StuCQL and StuEDM override students differently, any difference in students’ own subsequent choice-making is of interest independent of what actually gets delivered. We therefore examined students’ own stated choice (prior to any override) separately for the first choice-eligible problem in the training sequence—the second problem overall, since the first training problem carries no presentation-mode choice—and for the remaining nine. At the first choice-eligible problem, students in the two conditions chose similarly (StuEDM: 51.3% PS / 20.5% WE / 28.2% CPS; StuCQL: 56.4% PS / 15.4% WE / 28.2% CPS; $\chi^{2}(2)=0.38$ , $p=0.827$ ), confirming the two student populations began the study with statistically indistinguishable choice preferences. Across the remaining nine problems, however, students’ own choices diverged significantly between conditions (StuEDM: 62.7% PS / 21.9% WE / 15.4% CPS; StuCQL: 41.6% PS / 26.5% WE / 31.9% CPS; $\chi^{2}(2)=36.73$ , $p<.001$ ). Since the two populations were equivalent at the outset, this divergence is consistent with each policy’s differing override behavior reshaping students’ subsequent self-directed choices over the course of the training sequence, not merely the content ultimately delivered to them.

Decision distributions diverge sharply after override. Figure 7.1 compares each condition’s student-choice distribution against its final applied distribution. The two student populations’ own choices were broadly similar in rank order (PS the modal choice in both: 43.1% PS / 25.4% WE / 31.5% CPS for StuCQL’s students; 61.5% PS / 21.8% WE / 16.7% CPS for StuEDM’s students), but the delivered decisions diverge sharply: StuCQL delivers WE on 76.9% of decisions (PS 23.1%, CPS 0.0%), while StuEDM delivers PS on 82.3% of decisions (WE 16.2%, CPS 1.5%). For reference, Expert assigns all three modes near-uniformly (32.3% / 35.4% / 32.3%), confirming that this divergence is driven by the induced policies themselves rather than by differences in who was assigned to each condition. All three pairwise differences in the applied decision distribution are individually significant: StuEDM vs. StuCQL, $\chi^{2}(2)=290.57$ , $p<.001$ ; StuEDM vs. Expert, $\chi^{2}(2)=193.45$ , $p<.001$ ; StuCQL vs. Expert, $\chi^{2}(2)=175.60$ , $p<.001$ .

Two grouped bar charts. Left panel (StuCQL): student choice is 43 percent PS, 25 percent WE, 32 percent CPS, while the final delivered decision is 23 percent PS, 77 percent WE, 0 percent CPS. Right panel (StuEDM): student choice is 62 percent PS, 22 percent WE, 17 percent CPS, while the final delivered decision is 82 percent PS, 16 percent WE, 2 percent CPS.

Figure 7.1: Student’s own presentation-mode choice vs. the policy’s final (post-override) decision, for StuCQL and StuEDM.

StuCQL overrides far more often than StuEDM. Figure 7.2 shows that CQL overrode the student’s stated choice on 66.7% of decisions (260/390), whereas EDM overrode on only 28.2% (110/390)—a highly significant difference, $\chi^{2}(1)=114.15$ , $p<.001$ . This asymmetry is also visible at the student level: 12 of 39 StuCQL students were overridden on every single one of their 10 problems, while 13 of 39 StuEDM students were never overridden at all.

Stacked bar chart. StuCQL: 33 percent agreement, 67 percent override. StuEDM: 72 percent agreement, 28 percent override.

Figure 7.2: Agreement vs. override rate for StuCQL and StuEDM.

The two policies override toward opposite modes. Both conditions contributed the same 390 problem-level decisions (39 students $\times$ 10 problems); Table 7.2 restricts to the subset actually overridden in each condition (260/390 for StuCQL, 110/390 for StuEDM, per Figure 7.2) and breaks down what the student originally chose versus what the policy substituted. StuCQL’s overrides converge almost entirely on WE (85.8% of all its overrides land on WE, 14.2% on PS) and never once override toward CPS, regardless of the student’s original choice—CQL effectively eliminates the collaborative option from the delivered curriculum. StuEDM’s overrides converge instead on PS (81.8% of its overrides land on PS), but unlike CQL it does not eliminate any mode entirely: a small fraction of its overrides (2.7%) still route students toward CPS.

Both conditions contributed the same 390 decisions (39 students $\times$ 10 problems each; Section 7.5); this table restricts to the subset that was overridden — 260/390 for StuCQL and 110/390 for StuEDM, reflecting the agreement/override rates in Figure 7.2.
