## 7.6.5 Summary of Results

Across all five outcome measures, StuEDM produced the highest means. While three-group ANOVAs remained non-significant even with the augmented, comparably sized samples ( $N=26$ – $27$ per condition)—consistent with the modest effect sizes typical of single-semester classroom studies—the pattern of planned comparisons is directionally consistent but asymmetric across the two comparison conditions. StuEDM significantly outperformed Expert on four of five measures (adjusted posttest, NLG_post, NLG_post_iso, NLG_post_SR; post_iso approached significance, $p=0.056$ ). Against StuCQL, only the pretest-adjusted posttest comparison reached significance ( $p=0.048$ ); the remaining direct StuEDM-vs-StuCQL comparisons did not ( $p=0.067$ – $0.117$ ). StuEDM nonetheless significantly outperformed the combined average of Expert and StuCQL on four of five measures, with the fifth (post) approaching significance ( $p=0.051$ ). This pattern indicates that StuEDM’s advantage over the Expert baseline is the more robust finding in this study, while its advantage over StuCQL specifically is directionally consistent across all five measures but reaches significance only when pretest is covaried.

## 7.7 Discussion and Conclusions

We introduced StuEDM, the first application of Energy-based Distribution Matching (Jarrett et al. 2020a) to student-choice-with-override pedagogical policy induction in a deployed ITS. In StuEDM, students retain full autonomy over their presentation mode selection; the EDM-derived policy—learned from a substantially smaller, student-choice-only demonstration corpus—intervenes only when the student’s choice conflicts with the EDM-derived recommendation. We compared it against StuCQL (an analogous student-choice-with-override policy using CQL trained on a large interaction corpus) and Expert assignment in a classroom study on the Pyrenees probability tutor.

All policies support learning. Every condition produced large, significant pre-to-post gains (partial $\eta^{2}=0.29$ – $0.69$ ), confirming the robustness of the Pyrenees platform—consistent with prior evaluations (Ausin et al. 2020a; Hostetter et al. 2023c). The effect sizes were ordered StuEDM $>$ StuCQL $>$ Expert on both posttests, suggesting that adaptive policies confer an advantage even when all conditions learn.

EDM-based apprenticeship learning outperforms an expert-designed baseline, and trends ahead of reward-optimizing RL. StuEDM significantly outperformed a pretest-equivalent, size-matched Expert baseline on four of five measures, significantly outperformed StuCQL on the pretest-adjusted posttest, and outperformed the combined baseline on four of five measures—despite being trained on 244 students’ student-choice trajectories from five semesters, an order of magnitude fewer students and less than half the semester span of StuCQL’s 2,716-student, 13-semester training corpus. The direct StuEDM-vs-StuCQL comparison on the remaining four measures was directionally consistent with StuEDM’s advantage but did not reach significance ( $p=0.067$ – $0.117$ ); we report this honestly rather than emphasizing only the measures that happened to cross $\alpha=0.05$ . StuEDM also produced the highest engagement of the three conditions: StuEDM students spent significantly more time in the tutor than StuCQL students ( $p=0.008$ ; Section 7.6.3), while not differing from Expert on time on task. Rather than treating this as a confound to be explained away, we read it as a further engagement-related benefit of StuEDM: within a fixed homework-completion structure, students voluntarily chose to engage with the StuEDM-governed tutor for longer, alongside also learning more. The training-corpus-size advantage for the algorithm itself is a genuine efficiency gain at the data-collection level, independent of this engagement finding.

This pattern is noteworthy for two reasons. First, StuEDM’s robust advantage over an uninformed Expert baseline shows that occupancy-matching from a modest student-choice corpus is sufficient to produce a pedagogically meaningful policy, without reward engineering or a large interaction corpus. Second, its advantage over StuCQL—which already incorporates a conservative penalty to reduce distributional-shift overestimation—is directionally consistent across every measure we examined but is only significant once pretest is covaried; we treat this as suggestive rather than conclusive evidence that reward-free occupancy matching is a better-suited inductive bias than conservative reward-optimizing RL for this student-choice-with-override setting, and return to what would strengthen this claim in Limitations. EDM’s practical appeal remains regardless: many educational deployments cannot assemble the large, multi-year tutor-decision logs that RL-based policies require, but can still capture the smaller-scale, student-choice interaction data that offline apprenticeship learning needs.

The decision-level analysis in Section 7.6.4 offers a concrete behavioral account of this advantage. StuCQL overrode students on 66.7% of decisions—nearly the opposite of a system that merely nudges students at the margins—and its overrides converged almost entirely on Worked Examples while never once routing a student toward Collaborative Problem Solving, regardless of what the student had chosen. StuEDM, by contrast, overrode students on only 28.2% of decisions, respecting the student’s own choice more than twice as often, and when it did override it converged on Problem Solving while still preserving a small share of Collaborative Problem Solving assignments. A plausible interpretation is that StuCQL’s reward signal (delayed NLG) introduces noise from confounds unrelated to the override policy—student effort, test-taking strategy, or other factors that affect NLG but are outside the policy’s control—and that this noisy signal is amplified by how frequently CQL chooses to override, pushing the majority of students toward a single, more didactic mode (WE) largely irrespective of their own preference. EDM instead directly imitates the state-occupancy patterns of successful sessions, overriding far less often and, when it does, steering students toward more independent practice (PS) rather than passive instruction—a pattern more consistent with respecting student agency while still providing guidance. The position-based analysis in Section 7.6.4 reinforces this account: students in the two conditions chose equivalently at the very first choice-eligible problem, so the significant divergence in their own choices over the remaining nine problems is better explained by each policy’s differing override behavior reshaping subsequent student decision-making than by any pre-existing difference between the two student populations.

Design implications: override transparency and mode diversity. The override-transition asymmetry in Section 7.6.4 raises a design concern that learning-outcome measures alone do not capture: StuCQL did not merely override more often, it eliminated an entire pedagogical mode (Collaborative Problem Solving) from every student’s delivered curriculum, regardless of that student’s own preference—behaving, on this dimension, closer to an instructor-controlled policy than a student-choice-with-override one, despite nominally soliciting student input. This suggests two design directions for student-choice-with-override systems. First, override rate and delivered-mode coverage should be reported and monitored as first-class design metrics alongside learning outcomes, since a policy can win on learning gain while still eroding the autonomy guarantees the student-choice-with-override design was meant to provide—echoing recent CHI findings that users of adaptive systems consistently want more transparency into, and control over, the personalization applied to them, not just more accurate personalization (Wu et al. 2026). Second, systems could surface a lightweight rationale when overriding a student’s choice—particularly when a policy is about to override the large majority of students toward the same single mode, as StuCQL did toward Worked Examples—so students can distinguish a considered pedagogical intervention from a policy defaulting to one mode. Such rationales must be designed carefully, however: explanations for an automated recommendation do not reliably improve human-AI team performance and can instead increase uncritical acceptance of the recommendation regardless of its correctness (Bansal et al. 2021), so an override rationale should be evaluated for its effect on students’ decision-making, not deployed on the assumption that transparency alone helps. StuEDM’s higher agreement rate and more balanced override targets suggest that occupancy-matching objectives may be inherently more conservative interveners than reward-optimizing ones, a property worth preserving by design as these systems are extended to new domains.

Non-significance of three-group ANOVAs. The three-group ANOVAs were non-significant across all measures, consistent with the statistical challenges of detecting modest effect sizes ( $\eta^{2}\approx 0.04$ – $0.06$ ) even with comparably sized per-condition samples of $N=26$ – $27$ . The convergent pattern of one-sided planned comparisons, all in the expected direction and mostly statistically significant, is the appropriate evidence base for pre-specified directional hypotheses (Hostetter et al. 2023c).

Variance asymmetry and tail risk. A closer look at the within-condition spread clarifies why StuEDM’s large mean advantage does not translate into significance everywhere. On NLG_post, StuEDM’s distribution is comparatively tight ( $SD=0.338$ , minimum $=-0.08$ )—essentially no StuEDM student scored meaningfully worse on the posttest than the pretest. StuCQL ( $SD=0.496$ , minimum $=-1.00$ ) and especially Expert ( $SD=0.822$ , minimum $=-3.00$ ) both have a long left tail of students with substantially negative NLG. Because NLG’s denominator is $(1-\mathit{pre})$ , even a small posttest dip for a high-pretest student is amplified into an extreme negative value, and a handful of such students is enough to sharply inflate a condition’s variance. This asymmetry works against the omnibus and pairwise tests, whose standard errors are built from this inflated within-group variance, even though the point-estimate mean gap is large and consistently in StuEDM’s favor. Framed differently, StuEDM does not only win on average: it also appears to protect against the worst outcomes, with no student regressing meaningfully from pretest to posttest, whereas both comparison conditions include a minority of students who did. Whether this tail-risk reduction is a direct byproduct of imitating high-density state-occupancy patterns—and thus a distinct benefit of occupancy matching beyond the mean-outcome comparisons emphasized above—is a question we did not pre-register and cannot test with the same confirmatory rigor as the planned comparisons, but it is a pattern future work with larger samples should examine directly.

Implications for offline ITS policy design. Our results suggest that EDM-based offline apprenticeship learning is a compelling alternative to offline RL for student-choice-with-override policy induction. EDM requires no reward engineering, operates from a substantially smaller student-choice demonstration corpus, avoids distributional-shift overestimation, and produces policies compatible with student autonomy—a natural fit for settings where only student-choice interaction logs are available and reward specification is difficult. The student-choice-with-override design is important in its own right: it respects student agency while still providing adaptive guidance, which may contribute to engagement and motivation beyond pure learning gain (Chi et al. 2011b). Future work should investigate whether EDM’s advantage persists across multiple ITS domains, with varying demonstration corpus sizes, and when demonstration quality weighting is varied (e.g., sharper or flatter NLG-based weighting).

Limitations and future work. Several limitations apply. Per-condition sample sizes after ceiling exclusions are modest ( $N=26$ – $27$ ), limiting ANOVA power; this is likely why the direct StuEDM-vs-StuCQL comparison is directionally consistent across all five measures but reaches significance only for the pretest-adjusted posttest—the effect sizes are comparable to the significant StuEDM-vs-Expert comparisons, and a larger or multi-semester replication would give this comparison a fairer test. Expert’s analytic sample combines Spring 2026 students with a pretest-, materials-, and instrument-equivalent Spring 2025 cohort (Section 7.5.2); while every equivalence check we could run supported pooling, StuCQL and StuEDM reflect only Spring 2026, so residual semester-level confounds we could not test for (e.g., unmeasured cohort differences) cannot be fully ruled out for comparisons involving Expert. Relatedly, StuEDM students spent significantly more time engaging with the tutor than StuCQL students (Section 7.6.3); we treat this as a positive engagement outcome rather than a confound, but because time on task was not experimentally manipulated, future work should test whether higher voluntary engagement is a general property of occupancy-matching policies like EDM or specific to this deployment. The weighting of demonstrations for EDM training warrants further study: we weighted each demonstrated decision by a sigmoid transform of standardized NLG following the scheme used by Halide (Islam et al. 2026), but alternative schemes—hard Quantized Learning Gain filtering (Mao et al. 2018), different sigmoid sharpness, or per-decision (rather than per-trajectory) quality signals as in Halide’s decision-level extensions—may further improve performance. The sensitivity of EDM to demonstration corpus size and weighting is an open question. Finally, while we treat time on task as one engagement indicator, we did not measure other engagement or affective states (e.g., self-reported interest or frustration), which may further mediate the effects of the override mechanism on learning. Future work will assess StuEDM across multiple semesters, investigate combining EDM with interpretability techniques (Hostetter et al. 2023b) to explain override decisions to students, and explore whether personalized explanations (Hostetter et al. 2023c) can further amplify its learning gains.

## 7.8 Ethics and Privacy Statement

This study was conducted under an IRB-approved protocol at the authors’ institution. All student data (interaction logs, pretest and posttest scores) were collected as part of a normal homework assignment, de-identified prior to analysis, and stored securely; no personally identifiable information is reported in this chapter. Because StuCQL and StuEDM always honor a student’s own presentation-mode choice except in cases of policy disagreement, no student was denied instructional content or subjected to a fully automated decision without input into their own learning path. We do not anticipate societal risks beyond those already considered by the IRB review of the study design; the induced policies are specific to the Pyrenees probability tutor and are not intended for deployment outside of supervised educational research settings without further validation.

## Chapter 8 Conclusions and Future Work

## 8.1 Conclusions

This dissertation investigated the challenges of learning decision-making policies from offline, human-centric demonstration data, where reward functions are unknown, expert trajectories are scarce, and human behaviors are heterogeneous, evolving, and imperfect. We developed a progression of five apprenticeship learning frameworks, organized around two central challenges—reward modeling and demonstration quality—and closed with a real-world classroom deployment that tested whether these retrospective advances translate into live educational benefit.

On the reward modeling front, the first contribution adapted the EM–Energy-based Distribution Matching (EM-EDM) framework to relax the assumption that all demonstrations share a single, homogeneous reward function. By jointly clustering student trajectories by their latent reward functions and inducing cluster-specific policies over continuous state spaces, EM-EDM outperformed four state-of-the-art AL baselines and two DRL baselines on pedagogical action prediction with as few as 24 expert demonstrations, establishing that heterogeneous reward modeling is both feasible and data-efficient. The second contribution addressed the further assumption that reward functions remain fixed within a trajectory. THEMES automatically partitions expert trajectories into temporally coherent sub-trajectories via a hierarchically learned reward regulator and induces policies over each partition using EM-EDM. Validated across one educational and three real-world clinical healthcare datasets, THEMES consistently outperformed all flat and hierarchical AL baselines—achieving an AUC of 0.899 and a Jaccard of 0.653 using only 18 past-semester demonstrations to predict future-semester decisions—establishing that capturing temporally evolving reward structure substantially improves policy induction in human-centric domains.

On the demonstration quality front, the third contribution challenged the assumption that demonstrations are uniformly optimal. HALIDE integrates continuous, Normalized-Learning-Gain-derived quality signals directly into the hierarchical policy induction process, ranking rather than discarding imperfect demonstrations. HALIDE showed that incorporating suboptimal trajectories without quality-aware ranking degrades performance relative to unweighted hierarchical baselines, whereas ranked hierarchical learning enables principled reuse of mixed-quality data and yields consistent gains under cross-semester distribution shift—even among trajectories already labeled “expert.” The fourth contribution went further, challenging the assumption that a single trajectory-level quality rank is sufficient and that failed demonstrations carry no learnable signal. CCHAL redistributes trajectory-level demonstration quality to individual decisions via Bayesian credit assignment in feature-action space, separately scores each decision’s criticality by contrasting expert against non-expert behavior in a dual-arm design, and combines both signals to sharpen a Bradley-Terry pairwise contrastive IRL objective over observed failure trajectories. Evaluated across three clinical EHR datasets and one intelligent tutoring system dataset, CCHAL outperformed flat and hierarchical AL baselines, including its direct predecessor HALIDE, establishing that decision-level outcome attribution and behavioral criticality are both necessary to fully exploit demonstration quality signals beyond trajectory-level weighting alone.

The fifth contribution closed the loop with a real-world classroom deployment. StuEDM is a pedagogical policy in which students make their own decisions about how to engage with each training problem, and an EDM-derived policy—trained on only 244 students’ own pedagogical decisions across five semesters—intervenes only when it disagrees with the student’s choice. In a controlled classroom study with 104 undergraduate students, StuEDM significantly improved posttest performance and normalized learning gain over a pretest-equivalent Expert baseline that assigns among the same domain-expert-designed presentation modes with no adaptive guidance, and significantly improved the pretest-adjusted posttest score over StuCQL—an analogous policy using Conservative Q-Learning trained on a much larger, 2,716-student, 13-semester historical corpus. A decision-level behavioral analysis showed that StuEDM overrode students’ own decisions less than half as often as StuCQL, and that its overrides preserved more diversity across presentation modes, whereas StuCQL’s overrides converged almost entirely on a single mode. StuEDM students also engaged with the tutor significantly longer than StuCQL students, a further engagement-related outcome favoring StuEDM; the underlying training-corpus efficiency of the algorithm itself, an order of magnitude smaller and less than half the semester span of StuCQL’s, remains a genuine advantage regardless. Together, these results provide direct empirical evidence that offline AL from a substantially smaller, agency-driven demonstration corpus can rival reward-optimizing DRL trained on far larger corpora in a live educational deployment, while more directly respecting the student’s own pedagogical agency.

Together, these five contributions trace a progression from modeling heterogeneous, to evolving, to imperfect, to decision-level contrastive reward structures, culminating in real-world empirical validation. This dissertation establishes offline apprenticeship learning as a practical, data-efficient, and ethically viable paradigm for human-centric environments, offering scalable tools for understanding and optimizing sequential decision-making in settings where expert knowledge is limited, rewards are ambiguous, online interaction is infeasible or unethical, and demonstrations vary widely in quality—with demonstrated applications spanning education and clinical healthcare, and validated in both retrospective evaluation and a live classroom deployment.

## 8.2 Future Work

While this dissertation advances offline apprenticeship learning through heterogeneous, evolving, imperfect, and decision-level contrastive demonstration modeling, several promising directions remain.

A natural extension is to unify the separate mechanisms developed across chapters—hierarchical sub-trajectory partitioning, outcome-attributed decision weighting, dual-arm criticality scoring, and pairwise contrastive reward learning—into a single end-to-end framework, rather than the progressively layered structure adopted here. CCHAL’s dual-arm criticality mechanism, for instance, relies on sufficient neighbors in each contrast pool and a fixed number of MT-TICC clusters; adaptive neighborhood sizing, cluster-level criticality estimates, and per-fold GP credit inference would each reduce sensitivity to dataset size without per-dataset tuning. Similarly, HALIDE’s and StuEDM’s demonstration-quality weighting both rely on a single sigmoid-transformed quality signal; systematically varying the sharpness of this transform, or moving to per-decision rather than per-trajectory quality signals as CCHAL begins to explore, is an open direction for both educational and clinical applications.

The StuEDM classroom deployment raises its own concrete follow-ups. Because StuEDM students spent significantly more time on task than StuCQL students, future work should test EDM-induced policies under matched time budgets to isolate the algorithmic efficiency gain from the effect of additional study time. The single-semester classroom study should be extended to a multi-semester replication to give the directionally consistent, but not always significant, StuEDM-versus-StuCQL comparisons a fairer test at higher statistical power. More broadly, the decision-level finding that StuCQL overrides students’ choices far more often than StuEDM, and converges its overrides onto a single presentation mode, suggests that override rate and delivered-mode diversity should be reported as first-class design metrics for any policy that intervenes on a student’s own decisions, not just its learning-gain outcomes; future systems might also surface a lightweight rationale when overriding a student’s choice, evaluated directly for its effect on student decision-making rather than assumed to help.

Scaling these methods to high-dimensional and multi-modal human data—textual feedback, physiological signals, or richer interaction logs—represents an important avenue that will require improved representation learning and more robust energy-based optimization. Evaluation methodology is a second open area: current offline metrics may insufficiently capture human-centric objectives, motivating future work on causal off-policy estimators, counterfactual evaluation, and hybrid simulation–human studies that extend beyond the single live deployment demonstrated here. Integrating selective human-in-the-loop feedback, such as sparse preference queries or expert corrections, may further enhance model interpretability and reliability without requiring full online deployment. Finally, deeper theoretical analysis—covering generalization, identifiability, and stability for the energy-based and contrastive objectives developed across this dissertation—would strengthen its foundations and clarify the regimes under which these methods are reliable. Together, these directions offer a pathway toward more scalable, interpretable, agency-preserving, and trustworthy human-centric decision-making systems.

## Appendix A Acronyms

A summary of all acronyms is documented in Table A.1.

Table A.1: A summary of acronyms used in alphabetical order.

Acronym

Abbreviation

AL

Apprenticeship Learning

BC

Behavior Cloning

BCIL

Batch Contrastive Imitation Learning

BEAR

Bootstrapping Error Accumulation Reduction

CQL

Conservative Q-Learning

DRL

Deep Reinforcement Learning

DP

Dirichlet Process

DPO

Direct Preference Optimization

DQN

Deep Q-Network

EDM

Energy-based Distribution Matching

EM

Expectation–Maximization

EM-EDM

Expectation–Maximization Energy-based Distribution Matching

GAIL

Generative Adversarial Imitation Learning

HIRL

Hierarchical Inverse Reinforcement Learning

IL

Imitation Learning

IRL

Inverse Reinforcement Learning

ITS

Intelligent Tutoring System

MDP

Markov Decision Process

MCTS

Monte Carlo Tree Search

OILCA

Offline Imitation Learning with Counterfactual Augmentation

OPE

Off-Policy Evaluation

POMDP

Partially Observable Markov Decision Process

Q-function

Action–Value Function

RL

Reinforcement Learning

SPRINQL

State-Partitioned Robust Imitation via Q-Learning

TD

Temporal Difference

THEMES

Time-aware Hierarchical EM Energy-based Sub-trajectory Framework

VAE

Variational Autoencoder

## Appendix B Variables

A summary of all variables used throughout this dissertation is documented in Table B.1.

Table B.1: A summary of common variables and their definitions in alphabetical order.

Variable

Definition

 $a$ 

Action taken by the agent

 $A$ 

Action space

 $D$ 

Offline demonstration dataset

 $\mathcal{D}_{E}$ 

Set of expert demonstrations

 $\mathcal{D}_{I}$ 

Set of imperfect or mixed-quality demonstrations

 $E_{\theta}(\cdot)$ 

Energy function parameterized by $\theta$ 

 $\gamma$ 

Discount factor in reinforcement learning

 $H$ 

Horizon length of a trajectory

 $k$ 

Number of clusters or latent reward types

 $L(\cdot)$ 

Loss function

 $p(s^{\prime}|s,a)$ 

Transition probability from state $s$ to $s^{\prime}$ under action $a$ 

 $\pi(a|s)$ 

Policy: probability of selecting action $a$ in state $s$ 

 $\pi_{E}$ 

Expert policy

 $\pi_{\theta}$ 

Learned policy with parameters $\theta$ 

 $Q^{\pi}(s,a)$ 

Action–value function under policy $\pi$ 

 $r$ 

Reward value for a state–action pair

 $R(s,a)$ 

Reward function

 $R_{i}$ 

Reward function for cluster or intention $i$ 

 $s$ 

State variable

 $S$ 

State space

 $t$ 

Time step index

 $\tau$ 

Trajectory (sequence of state–action pairs)

 $\tau_{i}$ 

Sub-trajectory (used in THEMES and hierarchical AL)

 $V^{\pi}(s)$ 

Value function under policy $\pi$ 

 $w$ 

Importance weight or trajectory weight

 $\theta$ 

Policy or energy model parameters

 $\phi(\cdot)$ 

Feature representation or encoder function

 $Z$ 

Partition function / normalization constant (energy models)
